### Q1. MHT CET 2026 (20 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If $\vec{r} = 4\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 7\hat{k} + \lambda(3\hat{i} - 5\hat{k})$ is the vector equation of the line passing through $(7, 2, 2)$, where $\lambda$ is parameter, then the value of $\lambda$ is...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $-1$
- **(B)** $2$
- **(C)** $-2$
- **(D)** $1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The position vector of the given point is $\vec{r} = 7\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

Substituting this into the equation of the line:

$7\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k} = 4\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 7\hat{k} + \lambda(3\hat{i} - 5\hat{k})$

$7\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k} = (4 + 3\lambda)\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + (7 - 5\lambda)\hat{k}$

Comparing the coefficients of $\hat{i}$ and $\hat{k}$ on both sides:

$4 + 3\lambda = 7 \Rightarrow 3\lambda = 3 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$

$7 - 5\lambda = 2 \Rightarrow 5\lambda = 5 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$

The value of $\lambda$ is $1$.

Answer: $1$

</details>

---

### Q2. MHT CET 2026 (20 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular and the length of perpendicular drawn from $(0, 2, 3)$ to the line $\dfrac{x+3}{5} = \dfrac{y-1}{2} = \dfrac{z+4}{3}$ are respectively

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(2, 3, -1), \sqrt{31}$
- **(B)** $(2, 3, 1), \sqrt{21}$
- **(C)** $(2, 3, -1), \sqrt{21}$
- **(D)** $(2, 3, 1), \sqrt{31}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $(2, 3, -1), \sqrt{21}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given point be $P(0, 2, 3)$.

The equation of the given line is $\dfrac{x+3}{5} = \dfrac{y-1}{2} = \dfrac{z+4}{3} = \lambda$.

Any point on this line can be written as $Q(5\lambda - 3, 2\lambda + 1, 3\lambda - 4)$.

The direction ratios of the line segment $PQ$ are $(5\lambda - 3 - 0, 2\lambda + 1 - 2, 3\lambda - 4 - 3) = (5\lambda - 3, 2\lambda - 1, 3\lambda - 7)$.

Since $PQ$ is perpendicular to the given line, the dot product of their direction ratios must be zero:

$5(5\lambda - 3) + 2(2\lambda - 1) + 3(3\lambda - 7) = 0$

$25\lambda - 15 + 4\lambda - 2 + 9\lambda - 21 = 0$

$38\lambda - 38 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$

Substituting $\lambda = 1$ in the coordinates of $Q$, we get the foot of the perpendicular:

$Q(5(1) - 3, 2(1) + 1, 3(1) - 4) = (2, 3, -1)$

The length of the perpendicular is the distance between $P(0, 2, 3)$ and $Q(2, 3, -1)$:

$PQ = \sqrt{(2 - 0)^2 + (3 - 2)^2 + (-1 - 3)^2}$

$PQ = \sqrt{4 + 1 + 16} = \sqrt{21}$

Answer: $(2, 3, -1), \sqrt{21}$

</details>

### Q3. MHT CET 2026 (20 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The position vector of the point at which the line joining the points $\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}$ and $3\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$ cuts the plane $\vec{r}\cdot(\hat{j} - \hat{k}) = -1$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $-\dfrac{5}{3}\hat{i} - \dfrac{2}{3}\hat{j} + \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}$
- **(B)** $-\dfrac{5}{3}\hat{i} - \dfrac{2}{3}\hat{j} - \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{5}{3}\hat{i} - \dfrac{2}{3}\hat{j} + \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{5}{3}\hat{i} - \dfrac{2}{3}\hat{j} - \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $-\dfrac{5}{3}\hat{i} - \dfrac{2}{3}\hat{j} + \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given points be $\vec{a} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}$ and $\vec{b} = 3\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$.

The equation of the line passing through $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ is given by $\vec{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda(\vec{b} - \vec{a})$.

$\vec{b} - \vec{a} = (3 - 1)\hat{i} + (4 - 2)\hat{j} + (-2 - (-1))\hat{k} = 2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}$

$\vec{r} = (\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}) = (1 + 2\lambda)\hat{i} + (2 + 2\lambda)\hat{j} + (-1 - \lambda)\hat{k}$

The line intersects the plane $\vec{r}\cdot(\hat{j} - \hat{k}) = -1$. Substituting $\vec{r}$ into the plane equation:

$((1 + 2\lambda)\hat{i} + (2 + 2\lambda)\hat{j} + (-1 - \lambda)\hat{k}) \cdot (\hat{j} - \hat{k}) = -1$

$(2 + 2\lambda)(1) + (-1 - \lambda)(-1) = -1$

$2 + 2\lambda + 1 + \lambda = -1$

$3\lambda + 3 = -1 \Rightarrow \lambda = -\dfrac{4}{3}$

Substituting $\lambda = -\dfrac{4}{3}$ into the equation of the line:

$\vec{r} = \left(1 + 2\left(-\dfrac{4}{3}\right)\right)\hat{i} + \left(2 + 2\left(-\dfrac{4}{3}\right)\right)\hat{j} + \left(-1 - \left(-\dfrac{4}{3}\right)\right)\hat{k}$

$\vec{r} = -\dfrac{5}{3}\hat{i} - \dfrac{2}{3}\hat{j} + \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}$

Answer: $-\dfrac{5}{3}\hat{i} - \dfrac{2}{3}\hat{j} + \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}$

</details>

---

### Q4. MHT CET 2026 (19 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\dfrac{x-1}{1} = \dfrac{y-1}{2} = \dfrac{z-1}{3}$ and $\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-k}{2} = \dfrac{z-5}{1}$ are intersect at the point $P$, then the distance (in units) of the point $P$ from the origin $O(0, 0, 0)$ is ...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\sqrt{14}$
- **(B)** $\sqrt{21}$
- **(C)** $\sqrt{26}$
- **(D)** $\sqrt{29}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\sqrt{29}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the point on the first line be $P(\lambda + 1, 2\lambda + 1, 3\lambda + 1)$.

Let the point on the second line be $Q(\mu + 3, 2\mu + k, \mu + 5)$.

Since the lines intersect, for some $\lambda$ and $\mu$, the points $P$ and $Q$ must coincide.

Equating the $x$ and $z$ coordinates:

$\lambda + 1 = \mu + 3 \Rightarrow \lambda - \mu = 2$

$3\lambda + 1 = \mu + 5 \Rightarrow 3\lambda - \mu = 4$

Solving these two equations, we get $\lambda = 1$ and $\mu = -1$.

Substituting $\lambda = 1$ into the coordinates of $P$, we get the point of intersection as $(2, 3, 4)$.

The distance of this point from the origin $O(0, 0, 0)$ is:

$OP = \sqrt{2^2 + 3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9 + 16} = \sqrt{29}$

Answer: $\sqrt{29}$

</details>

---

### Q5. MHT CET 2026 (19 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The distance of the point $(1,1,1)$ from the line passing through the point $(2,3,4)$ whose direction ratios are $2, -3, 6$ is:

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{\sqrt{290}}{7}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{\sqrt{390}}{7}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{\sqrt{490}}{7}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{\sqrt{590}}{7}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\dfrac{\sqrt{490}}{7}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given point be $P(1, 1, 1)$ and the line pass through the point $A(2, 3, 4)$ with direction vector $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$.

The vector joining $A$ to $P$ is $\overrightarrow{AP} = (1-2)\hat{i} + (1-3)\hat{j} + (1-4)\hat{k} = -\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}$.

The perpendicular distance $d$ of the point from the line is given by the formula $d = \dfrac{|\overrightarrow{AP} \times \vec{b}|}{|\vec{b}|}$.

Calculating the cross product:
$\overrightarrow{AP} \times \vec{b} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ -1 & -2 & -3 \\ 2 & -3 & 6 \end{vmatrix}$

$\overrightarrow{AP} \times \vec{b} = \hat{i}(-12 - 9) - \hat{j}(-6 - (-6)) + \hat{k}(3 - (-4))$

$\overrightarrow{AP} \times \vec{b} = -21\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 7\hat{k}$

The magnitude of the cross product is:
$|\overrightarrow{AP} \times \vec{b}| = \sqrt{(-21)^2 + 0^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{441 + 49} = \sqrt{490}$

The magnitude of the direction vector is:
$|\vec{b}| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-3)^2 + 6^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9 + 36} = \sqrt{49} = 7$

Therefore, the distance is:
$d = \dfrac{\sqrt{490}}{7}$

Answer: $\dfrac{\sqrt{490}}{7}$

</details>

### Q6. MHT CET 2026 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\dfrac{1-x}{3} = \dfrac{3y-1}{2\lambda+1} = 4 - z$ and $\dfrac{x+2}{2\mu} = \dfrac{1-2y}{6} = \dfrac{5-z}{7}$ are perpendicular, then $3\mu + \lambda = \ldots$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $-4$
- **(B)** $3$
- **(C)** $8$
- **(D)** $6$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $3$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Rewriting the equations of the lines in standard symmetric form $\dfrac{x-x_1}{a} = \dfrac{y-y_1}{b} = \dfrac{z-z_1}{c}$:

For the first line:
$\dfrac{x-1}{-3} = \dfrac{y-1/3}{(2\lambda+1)/3} = \dfrac{z-4}{-1}$
The direction ratios are $a_1 = -3$, $b_1 = \dfrac{2\lambda+1}{3}$, $c_1 = -1$.

For the second line:
$\dfrac{x+2}{2\mu} = \dfrac{y-1/2}{-3} = \dfrac{z-5}{-7}$
The direction ratios are $a_2 = 2\mu$, $b_2 = -3$, $c_2 = -7$.

Since the two lines are perpendicular, the dot product of their direction vectors is zero:
$a_1 a_2 + b_1 b_2 + c_1 c_2 = 0$

Substituting the direction ratios:
$(-3)(2\mu) + \left(\dfrac{2\lambda+1}{3}\right)(-3) + (-1)(-7) = 0$

$-6\mu - (2\lambda+1) + 7 = 0$

$-6\mu - 2\lambda + 6 = 0$

$6\mu + 2\lambda = 6$

$3\mu + \lambda = 3$

Answer: $3$

</details>

### Q7. MHT CET 2026 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Consider the lines
$L_1: \dfrac{x}{2} = \dfrac{y}{2} = -z,$
$L_2: \dfrac{x-1}{1} = \dfrac{y-1}{2} = \dfrac{z-3}{2}.$
The unit vector perpendicular to both $L_1$ and $L_2$ is:

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{-6\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{65}}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{6\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{65}}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{6\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{65}}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{6\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{65}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\dfrac{6\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{65}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction vector of line $L_1$ is $\vec{v}_1 = 2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}$.

The direction vector of line $L_2$ is $\vec{v}_2 = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

A vector perpendicular to both lines is given by their cross product $\vec{v}_1 \times \vec{v}_2$.

$\vec{v}_1 \times \vec{v}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 2 & 2 & -1 \\ 1 & 2 & 2 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{v}_1 \times \vec{v}_2 = \hat{i}(4 - (-2)) - \hat{j}(4 - (-1)) + \hat{k}(4 - 2)$

$\vec{v}_1 \times \vec{v}_2 = 6\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$

The magnitude of this vector is:

$|\vec{v}_1 \times \vec{v}_2| = \sqrt{6^2 + (-5)^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{36 + 25 + 4} = \sqrt{65}$

The unit vector perpendicular to both $L_1$ and $L_2$ is:

$\dfrac{6\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{65}}$

Answer: $\dfrac{6\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}}{\sqrt{65}}$

</details>

---

### Q8. MHT CET 2026 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular drawn from the point $P(-1, 3, 2)$ to the line formed by the intersection of the planes $x + y + z = 1$ and $2x - y + z = 2$ are:

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\left(0, -\dfrac{1}{2}, \dfrac{3}{2}\right)$
- **(B)** $(1, 0, 0)$
- **(C)** $\left(-\dfrac{1}{2}, 0, \dfrac{3}{2}\right)$
- **(D)** $\left(0, \dfrac{1}{2}, -\dfrac{3}{2}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\left(0, -\dfrac{1}{2}, \dfrac{3}{2}\right)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the line of intersection of the given planes be $L$. The direction vector $\vec{d}$ of the line $L$ is given by the cross product of the normal vectors of the two planes.

$\vec{d} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 2 & -1 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = \hat{i}(1 - (-1)) - \hat{j}(1 - 2) + \hat{k}(-1 - 2) = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j} - 3\hat{k}$

To find a point on the line $L$, we can set $y = 0$ in the equations of the planes:
$x + z = 1$
$2x + z = 2$

Subtracting the first equation from the second gives $x = 1$. Substituting $x = 1$ into the first equation gives $z = 0$. Thus, a point on the line is $(1, 0, 0)$.

The equation of the line $L$ in symmetric form is:
$\dfrac{x - 1}{2} = \dfrac{y - 0}{1} = \dfrac{z - 0}{-3} = \lambda$

A general point on the line $L$ is $Q(2\lambda + 1, \lambda, -3\lambda)$.

The direction ratios of the line segment $PQ$ joining $P(-1, 3, 2)$ and $Q$ are:
$\vec{PQ} = (2\lambda + 1 - (-1))\hat{i} + (\lambda - 3)\hat{j} + (-3\lambda - 2)\hat{k} = (2\lambda + 2)\hat{i} + (\lambda - 3)\hat{j} + (-3\lambda - 2)\hat{k}$

Since $PQ$ is perpendicular to the line $L$, the dot product of $\vec{PQ}$ and $\vec{d}$ must be zero:
$\vec{PQ} \cdot \vec{d} = 0$
$2(2\lambda + 2) + 1(\lambda - 3) - 3(-3\lambda - 2) = 0$
$4\lambda + 4 + \lambda - 3 + 9\lambda + 6 = 0$
$14\lambda + 7 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = -\dfrac{1}{2}$

Substituting $\lambda = -\dfrac{1}{2}$ into the coordinates of $Q$, we get the foot of the perpendicular:
$x = 2\left(-\dfrac{1}{2}\right) + 1 = 0$
$y = -\dfrac{1}{2}$
$z = -3\left(-\dfrac{1}{2}\right) = \dfrac{3}{2}$

The coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular are $\left(0, -\dfrac{1}{2}, \dfrac{3}{2}\right)$.

Answer: $\left(0, -\dfrac{1}{2}, \dfrac{3}{2}\right)$

</details>

---

### Q9. MHT CET 2026 (14 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The equation of the line passing through the point of intersection of the lines $\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-5}{2} = \dfrac{z-1}{-1}$ & $\dfrac{x-4}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-1} = \dfrac{z-4}{2}$ and in the direction of $\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\bar{r} = (2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$
- **(B)** $\bar{r} = (2\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$
- **(C)** $\bar{r} = (2\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$
- **(D)** $\bar{r} = (2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\bar{r} = (2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the point of intersection be $P$. Any point on the first line is given by $(s+3, 2s+5, -s+1)$ and on the second line by $(2t+4, -t+2, 2t+4)$.

For the point of intersection, these coordinates must be equal:

$s+3 = 2t+4 \Rightarrow s - 2t = 1$

$2s+5 = -t+2 \Rightarrow 2s + t = -3$

$-s+1 = 2t+4 \Rightarrow s + 2t = -3$

Solving $s - 2t = 1$ and $s + 2t = -3$, we get $2s = -2 \Rightarrow s = -1$.

Substituting $s = -1$ in $s - 2t = 1$, we get $-1 - 2t = 1 \Rightarrow t = -1$.

These values satisfy the second equation $2(-1) + (-1) = -3$.

Substituting $s = -1$ into the coordinates of the first line, the point of intersection is $(2, 3, 2)$.

The position vector of this point is $2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

The required line passes through this point and is parallel to the vector $\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

The equation of the line is $\bar{r} = (2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$.

Answer: $\bar{r} = (2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

</details>

---

### Q10. MHT CET 2026 (14 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The Cartesian equation of the line passing through $A(3, 2, 1)$ and $B(1, 3, 1)$ is:

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{x-3}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-1},\ z = 1$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{x-3}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-1},\ z = -1$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{x-3}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{1},\ z = -1$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{x-3}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{1},\ z = -1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\dfrac{x-3}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-1},\ z = 1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the line passing through $A(3, 2, 1)$ and $B(1, 3, 1)$ are $3 - 1, 2 - 3, 1 - 1$, which are $2, -1, 0$.

The equation of the line passing through $A(3, 2, 1)$ with direction ratios $2, -1, 0$ is given by:

$\dfrac{x - 3}{2} = \dfrac{y - 2}{-1} = \dfrac{z - 1}{0}$

This implies $\dfrac{x - 3}{2} = \dfrac{y - 2}{-1}$ and $z - 1 = 0$, which gives $z = 1$.

Therefore, the Cartesian equation of the line is $\dfrac{x - 3}{2} = \dfrac{y - 2}{-1}, z = 1$.

Answer: $\dfrac{x-3}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-1},\ z = 1$

</details>

### Q11. MHT CET 2026 (13 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $M$ is the foot of the perpendicular drawn from $A(4,3,2)$ on the line joining the points $B(2,4,1)$ and $C(4,5,3)$, the co-ordinates of $M$ are _____

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\left(\dfrac{41}{9}, \dfrac{28}{9}, \dfrac{19}{9}\right)$
- **(B)** $\left(\dfrac{28}{9}, \dfrac{41}{9}, \dfrac{19}{9}\right)$
- **(C)** $\left(\dfrac{9}{28}, \dfrac{9}{41}, \dfrac{9}{19}\right)$
- **(D)** $\left(\dfrac{19}{9}, \dfrac{41}{9}, \dfrac{28}{9}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\left(\dfrac{28}{9}, \dfrac{41}{9}, \dfrac{19}{9}\right)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the line joining $B(2,4,1)$ and $C(4,5,3)$ are $(4-2, 5-4, 3-1) = (2, 1, 2)$.

The equation of the line $BC$ is given by:
$\dfrac{x - 2}{2} = \dfrac{y - 4}{1} = \dfrac{z - 1}{2} = \lambda$

Any point $M$ on the line $BC$ has coordinates $(2\lambda + 2, \lambda + 4, 2\lambda + 1)$.

The direction ratios of the line segment $AM$ joining $A(4,3,2)$ and $M$ are:
$(2\lambda + 2 - 4, \lambda + 4 - 3, 2\lambda + 1 - 2) = (2\lambda - 2, \lambda + 1, 2\lambda - 1)$

Since $AM$ is perpendicular to the line $BC$, the dot product of their direction ratios is zero:
$2(2\lambda - 2) + 1(\lambda + 1) + 2(2\lambda - 1) = 0$

$4\lambda - 4 + \lambda + 1 + 4\lambda - 2 = 0$

$9\lambda - 5 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = \dfrac{5}{9}$

Substituting $\lambda = \dfrac{5}{9}$ into the coordinates of $M$, we get:
$x = 2\left(\dfrac{5}{9}\right) + 2 = \dfrac{10}{9} + \dfrac{18}{9} = \dfrac{28}{9}$

$y = \dfrac{5}{9} + 4 = \dfrac{5}{9} + \dfrac{36}{9} = \dfrac{41}{9}$

$z = 2\left(\dfrac{5}{9}\right) + 1 = \dfrac{10}{9} + \dfrac{9}{9} = \dfrac{19}{9}$

The coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular $M$ are $\left(\dfrac{28}{9}, \dfrac{41}{9}, \dfrac{19}{9}\right)$.

Answer: $\left(\dfrac{28}{9}, \dfrac{41}{9}, \dfrac{19}{9}\right)$

</details>

---

### Q12. MHT CET 2026 (13 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The plane passing through the point $(5, 1, 2)$ and perpendicular to the line $2x - 4 = y - 4 = z - 5$ will meet the line at the point

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(3, 2, 2)$
- **(B)** $(3, 2, 1)$
- **(C)** $(2, 3, 1)$
- **(D)** $(1, 2, 3)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $(1, 2, 3)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the given line can be rewritten in standard form as:

$\dfrac{x - 2}{1/2} = \dfrac{y - 4}{1} = \dfrac{z - 5}{1}$

Multiplying the denominators by $2$, we get:

$\dfrac{x - 2}{1} = \dfrac{y - 4}{2} = \dfrac{z - 5}{2}$

The direction ratios of the line are $(1, 2, 2)$. Since the plane is perpendicular to this line, the normal vector to the plane has direction ratios $(1, 2, 2)$.

The equation of the plane passing through the point $(5, 1, 2)$ is:

$1(x - 5) + 2(y - 1) + 2(z - 2) = 0$

$x + 2y + 2z - 11 = 0$

Let the general point on the line be $(t + 2, 2t + 4, 2t + 5)$. Since this point lies on the plane, we substitute it into the plane's equation:

$(t + 2) + 2(2t + 4) + 2(2t + 5) - 11 = 0$

$t + 2 + 4t + 8 + 4t + 10 - 11 = 0$

$9t + 9 = 0$

$t = -1$

Substituting $t = -1$ back into the general point, we get the coordinates of the point of intersection:

$x = -1 + 2 = 1$

$y = 2(-1) + 4 = 2$

$z = 2(-1) + 5 = 3$

The plane meets the line at the point $(1, 2, 3)$.

Answer: $(1, 2, 3)$

</details>

### Q13. MHT CET 2026 (13 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let the line $\dfrac{x - 2}{3} = \dfrac{y - 1}{-5} = \dfrac{z + 2}{2}$ lie in the plane $x + 3y - \alpha z + \beta = 0$. Then $(\alpha, \beta)$ equals

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(5, -15)$
- **(B)** $(-5, 5)$
- **(C)** $(6, -17)$
- **(D)** $(-6, 7)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $(-6, 7)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Since the line lies in the plane, the normal vector of the plane is perpendicular to the direction vector of the line.

Direction vector of the line is $3\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

Normal vector of the plane is $\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - \alpha\hat{k}$.

Taking the dot product to be zero:

$3(1) - 5(3) + 2(-\alpha) = 0$

$3 - 15 - 2\alpha = 0$

$-12 - 2\alpha = 0 \Rightarrow \alpha = -6$

Also, the point $(2, 1, -2)$ on the line must satisfy the equation of the plane.

Substituting $(2, 1, -2)$ in $x + 3y - \alpha z + \beta = 0$:

$2 + 3(1) - \alpha(-2) + \beta = 0$

$5 + 2\alpha + \beta = 0$

Substituting $\alpha = -6$:

$5 + 2(-6) + \beta = 0$

$5 - 12 + \beta = 0 \Rightarrow \beta = 7$

Therefore, $(\alpha, \beta) = (-6, 7)$.

Answer: $(-6, 7)$

</details>

---

### Q14. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The length of the perpendicular from the point $(3, 2, 1)$ to the line $\dfrac{x-7}{-2} = \dfrac{y-7}{2} = \dfrac{z-6}{3}$ is $k$ units. then the value of $k$....

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3$
- **(B)** $5$
- **(C)** $7$
- **(D)** $9$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $7$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given point be $P(3, 2, 1)$ and the given line be $\dfrac{x-7}{-2} = \dfrac{y-7}{2} = \dfrac{z-6}{3} = \lambda$.

Any point on the line can be represented as $Q(-2\lambda + 7, 2\lambda + 7, 3\lambda + 6)$.

The direction ratios of the line segment $PQ$ are $(-2\lambda + 4, 2\lambda + 5, 3\lambda + 5)$.

Since $PQ$ is perpendicular to the given line, the dot product of their direction ratios is zero:

$-2(-2\lambda + 4) + 2(2\lambda + 5) + 3(3\lambda + 5) = 0$

$4\lambda - 8 + 4\lambda + 10 + 9\lambda + 15 = 0$

$17\lambda + 17 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = -1$

Substituting $\lambda = -1$, the coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular $Q$ are $(9, 5, 3)$.

The length of the perpendicular $k$ is the distance $PQ$:

$k = \sqrt{(9 - 3)^2 + (5 - 2)^2 + (3 - 1)^2}$

$k = \sqrt{36 + 9 + 4} = \sqrt{49} = 7$

Answer: $7$

</details>

---

### Q15. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The vector equation of the line passing through the point $(1, 2, -4)$ and perpendicular to the two the lines $\dfrac{x-8}{3} = \dfrac{y+19}{-16} = \dfrac{z-10}{7}$ and $\dfrac{x-5}{3} = \dfrac{y-33}{8} = \dfrac{5-z}{5}$ is...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\bar{r} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k} + \lambda(5\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} + 7\hat{k})$
- **(B)** $\bar{r} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k} + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 6\hat{k})$
- **(C)** $\bar{r} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k} + \lambda(3\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 13\hat{k})$
- **(D)** $\bar{r} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k} + \lambda(4\hat{i} + 10\hat{j} + 9\hat{k})$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\bar{r} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k} + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 6\hat{k})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the first line are $3, -16, 7$.

The second line can be rewritten as $\dfrac{x-5}{3} = \dfrac{y-33}{8} = \dfrac{z-5}{-5}$.

The direction ratios of the second line are $3, 8, -5$.

The required line is perpendicular to both given lines, so its direction vector $\vec{b}$ is parallel to the cross product of their direction vectors.

$\vec{b} = (3\hat{i} - 16\hat{j} + 7\hat{k}) \times (3\hat{i} + 8\hat{j} - 5\hat{k})$

$\vec{b} = \hat{i}(80 - 56) - \hat{j}(-15 - 21) + \hat{k}(24 + 48)$

$\vec{b} = 24\hat{i} + 36\hat{j} + 72\hat{k}$

Dividing by $12$, the direction ratios of the required line are proportional to $2, 3, 6$.

The line passes through the point $(1, 2, -4)$, so its position vector is $\vec{a} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k}$.

The vector equation of the line is $\bar{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda\vec{b}$.

$\bar{r} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k} + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 6\hat{k})$

Answer: $\bar{r} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 4\hat{k} + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 6\hat{k})$

</details>

---

### Q16. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the line $\dfrac{x-2}{3} = \dfrac{y-1}{5} = \dfrac{z+2}{2}$ lies in the plane $x + 3y - pz + q = 0$, then the value of $p - q = $.........

**Options:**

- **(A)** $10$
- **(B)** $9$
- **(C)** $22$
- **(D)** $32$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $32$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Since the given line lies in the plane, the direction vector of the line is perpendicular to the normal vector of the plane.

Direction vector of the line is $3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$ and the normal vector of the plane is $\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - p\hat{k}$.

Taking their dot product as zero:

$3(1) + 5(3) + 2(-p) = 0$

$18 - 2p = 0 \Rightarrow p = 9$

Also, any point on the line must satisfy the equation of the plane. A point on the line is $(2, 1, -2)$.

Substituting this point into the plane equation:

$2 + 3(1) - p(-2) + q = 0$

$5 + 2p + q = 0$

Substituting $p = 9$ into the equation:

$5 + 2(9) + q = 0$

$23 + q = 0 \Rightarrow q = -23$

Therefore, the value of $p - q$ is:

$p - q = 9 - (-23) = 32$

Answer: $32$

</details>

### Q17. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A line passing through $A(1, 3, 2)$ and parallel to the line $\dfrac{x-2}{1}=\dfrac{y-4}{2}=\dfrac{z-6}{1}$ intersects the plane $x-y+3z-6=0$ at point B. Another line through B and perpendicular to the plane $x-y+3z-6=0$ intersects the plane $2x-y+z+4=0$ at the point C. Then the equation of median of $\Delta ABC$ through the vertex A is ...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{x-1}{5}=\dfrac{y-3}{1}=\dfrac{z-2}{-1}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{x-1}{1}=\dfrac{y-3}{5}=\dfrac{z-2}{-1}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y-3}{3}=\dfrac{z-2}{5}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{x-1}{3}=\dfrac{y-3}{-5}=\dfrac{z-2}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\dfrac{x-1}{1}=\dfrac{y-3}{5}=\dfrac{z-2}{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the line passing through $A(1, 3, 2)$ and parallel to $\dfrac{x-2}{1}=\dfrac{y-4}{2}=\dfrac{z-6}{1}$ is given by:

$\dfrac{x-1}{1}=\dfrac{y-3}{2}=\dfrac{z-2}{1} = r$

Any point on this line is of the form $(r+1, 2r+3, r+2)$. Since this line intersects the plane $x-y+3z-6=0$ at point B, we substitute the coordinates into the plane's equation:

$(r+1) - (2r+3) + 3(r+2) - 6 = 0$

$2r - 2 = 0 \Rightarrow r = 1$

Thus, the coordinates of point B are $(2, 5, 3)$.

The line passing through B and perpendicular to the plane $x-y+3z-6=0$ has direction ratios proportional to the normal of the plane, which are $(1, -1, 3)$. The equation of this line is:

$\dfrac{x-2}{1}=\dfrac{y-5}{-1}=\dfrac{z-3}{3} = k$

Any point on this line is $(k+2, -k+5, 3k+3)$. This line intersects the plane $2x-y+z+4=0$ at point C. Substituting the coordinates into the plane's equation:

$2(k+2) - (-k+5) + (3k+3) + 4 = 0$

$6k + 6 = 0 \Rightarrow k = -1$

Thus, the coordinates of point C are $(1, 6, 0)$.

To find the equation of the median of $\Delta ABC$ through vertex A, we first find the midpoint M of the side BC. The coordinates of M are:

$\left(\dfrac{2+1}{2}, \dfrac{5+6}{2}, \dfrac{3+0}{2}\right) = \left(\dfrac{3}{2}, \dfrac{11}{2}, \dfrac{3}{2}\right)$

The direction ratios of the median AM are:

$\left(\dfrac{3}{2} - 1, \dfrac{11}{2} - 3, \dfrac{3}{2} - 2\right) = \left(\dfrac{1}{2}, \dfrac{5}{2}, -\dfrac{1}{2}\right)$

Multiplying by 2, the direction ratios are $(1, 5, -1)$.

Therefore, the equation of the median through A is:

$\dfrac{x-1}{1}=\dfrac{y-3}{5}=\dfrac{z-2}{-1}$

Answer: $\dfrac{x-1}{1}=\dfrac{y-3}{5}=\dfrac{z-2}{-1}$

</details>

---

### Q18. MHT CET 2026 (12 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\bar{r}=(\hat{i}-\hat{j}+2\hat{k})+\lambda(\hat{i}+2\hat{k})$ and $\dfrac{x-p}{2}=y+2=z+1$ intersect, then the value of $p$ is ...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $-1$
- **(B)** $1$
- **(C)** $-2$
- **(D)** $2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $-2$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The first line is given by $\vec{r} = (\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} + 2\hat{k})$.

Any point on this line can be written as $(1+\lambda, -1, 2+2\lambda)$.

The second line is $\dfrac{x-p}{2} = \dfrac{y+2}{1} = \dfrac{z+1}{1} = \mu$.

Any point on this line can be written as $(2\mu+p, \mu-2, \mu-1)$.

For the lines to intersect, their coordinates must be equal for some values of $\lambda$ and $\mu$.

Equating the $y$-coordinates:

$-1 = \mu - 2 \Rightarrow \mu = 1$

Equating the $z$-coordinates:

$2 + 2\lambda = \mu - 1$

Substituting $\mu = 1$:

$2 + 2\lambda = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = -1$

Equating the $x$-coordinates:

$1 + \lambda = 2\mu + p$

Substituting $\lambda = -1$ and $\mu = 1$:

$1 - 1 = 2(1) + p \Rightarrow 0 = 2 + p \Rightarrow p = -2$

Answer: $-2$

</details>

---

### Q19. MHT CET 2026 (20 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Lines $\vec{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda\vec{b}$ and $\vec{r} = \vec{b} + \mu\vec{a}$ intersect at point $(2, 4, -4)$. If $|\vec{a} - \vec{b}| = 4$, then $\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b} =$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $5$
- **(B)** $10$
- **(C)** $-5$
- **(D)** $-10$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $5$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the point of intersection be $\vec{p} = 2\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} - 4\hat{k}$.

Since the lines intersect at $\vec{p}$, there exist scalars $\lambda$ and $\mu$ such that:

$\vec{a} + \lambda\vec{b} = \vec{b} + \mu\vec{a}$

$\Rightarrow (1 - \mu)\vec{a} + (\lambda - 1)\vec{b} = \vec{0}$

Assuming $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$ are non-collinear, we get $1 - \mu = 0$ and $\lambda - 1 = 0$, which gives $\lambda = 1$ and $\mu = 1$.

Thus, the position vector of the point of intersection is:

$\vec{p} = \vec{a} + \vec{b}$

The magnitude squared of $\vec{p}$ is:

$|\vec{a} + \vec{b}|^2 = 2^2 + 4^2 + (-4)^2 = 4 + 16 + 16 = 36$

We are given $|\vec{a} - \vec{b}| = 4$, so:

$|\vec{a} - \vec{b}|^2 = 16$

Using the vector identity:

$|\vec{a} + \vec{b}|^2 - |\vec{a} - \vec{b}|^2 = 4\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b}$

Substituting the values:

$36 - 16 = 4\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b}$

$20 = 4\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b}$

$\vec{a} \cdot \vec{b} = 5$

Answer: $5$

</details>

---

**Options:**

- **(B)** $x = 0$
- **(C)** $x + y + z = 2$

<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

The line passes through the point $A(0, 1, 1)$ and is parallel to the X-axis. Therefore, its direction ratios are $1, 0, 0$.

The Cartesian equations of the line are given by:

$\dfrac{x - 0}{1} = \dfrac{y - 1}{0} = \dfrac{z - 1}{0}$

This implies $y - 1 = 0$ and $z - 1 = 0$.

Thus, the equations of the line are $y = 1, z = 1$.

Answer: $y = 1, z = 1$

### Q21. MHT CET 2026 (20 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The shortest distance between the lines $\dfrac{x - 3}{3} = \dfrac{y - 8}{-1} = \dfrac{z - 3}{1}$ and $\dfrac{x + 3}{-3} = \dfrac{y + 7}{2} = \dfrac{z - 6}{4}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $5\sqrt{30}$
- **(B)** $3\sqrt{30}$
- **(C)** $2\sqrt{30}$
- **(D)** $\sqrt{30}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $3\sqrt{30}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given lines are:

$L_1: \dfrac{x - 3}{3} = \dfrac{y - 8}{-1} = \dfrac{z - 3}{1}$

$L_2: \dfrac{x + 3}{-3} = \dfrac{y + 7}{2} = \dfrac{z - 6}{4}$

The position vectors of points on the lines are $\vec{a}_1 = 3\hat{i} + 8\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$ and $\vec{a}_2 = -3\hat{i} - 7\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$.

The direction vectors of the lines are $\vec{b}_1 = 3\hat{i} - \hat{j} + \hat{k}$ and $\vec{b}_2 = -3\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$.

The shortest distance $d$ between two skew lines is given by:

$d = \dfrac{|(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)|}{|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2|}$

First, finding $\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1$:

$\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1 = (-3 - 3)\hat{i} + (-7 - 8)\hat{j} + (6 - 3)\hat{k} = -6\hat{i} - 15\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$

Next, finding $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$:

$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 3 & -1 & 1 \\ -3 & 2 & 4 \end{vmatrix}$

$= \hat{i}(-4 - 2) - \hat{j}(12 - (-3)) + \hat{k}(6 - 3)$

$= -6\hat{i} - 15\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$

Now, finding the dot product $(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)$:

$(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2) = (-6)(-6) + (-15)(-15) + (3)(3)$

$= 36 + 225 + 9 = 270$

Now, finding the magnitude $|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2|$:

$|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2| = \sqrt{(-6)^2 + (-15)^2 + 3^2}$

$= \sqrt{36 + 225 + 9} = \sqrt{270}$

Finally, calculating the shortest distance $d$:

$d = \dfrac{270}{\sqrt{270}} = \sqrt{270} = \sqrt{9 \times 30} = 3\sqrt{30}$

Answer: $3\sqrt{30}$

</details>

---

### Q22. MHT CET 2026 (19 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The shortest distance between the lines $\vec{r} = (4\hat{i} - \hat{j}) + \lambda(\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k})$ and $\vec{r} = (\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) + \mu(\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} - 5\hat{k})$ is...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{1}{2}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{1}{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given lines are $\vec{r} = \vec{a}_1 + \lambda \vec{b}_1$ and $\vec{r} = \vec{a}_2 + \mu \vec{b}_2$, where:
$\vec{a}_1 = 4\hat{i} - \hat{j}$
$\vec{b}_1 = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}$
$\vec{a}_2 = \hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$
$\vec{b}_2 = \hat{i} + 4\hat{j} - 5\hat{k}$

The shortest distance $d$ between two skew lines is given by:
$d = \dfrac{|(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)|}{|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2|}$

First, find $\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1$:
$\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1 = (\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) - (4\hat{i} - \hat{j}) = -3\hat{i} + 2\hat{k}$

Next, find the cross product $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$:
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 1 & 2 & -3 \\ 1 & 4 & -5 \end{vmatrix}$
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \hat{i}(-10 + 12) - \hat{j}(-5 + 3) + \hat{k}(4 - 2) = 2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$

Find the magnitude of $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$:
$|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2| = \sqrt{2^2 + 2^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{12} = 2\sqrt{3}$

Now, find the dot product $(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)$:
$(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2) = (-3)(2) + (0)(2) + (2)(2) = -6 + 4 = -2$

Finally, calculate the shortest distance $d$:
$d = \dfrac{|-2|}{2\sqrt{3}} = \dfrac{2}{2\sqrt{3}} = \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$

Answer: $\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}$

</details>

---

### Q23. MHT CET 2026 (19 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A line passing through the points $(1, -1, 2)$ and $(2, 0, 1)$ meets the XY plane and the YZ plane at points A and B respectively. The distance AB is equal to...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2\sqrt{2}$
- **(B)** $2\sqrt{3}$
- **(C)** $3\sqrt{2}$
- **(D)** $3\sqrt{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $3\sqrt{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the line passing through $(1, -1, 2)$ and $(2, 0, 1)$ is given by:

$\dfrac{x - 1}{2 - 1} = \dfrac{y - (-1)}{0 - (-1)} = \dfrac{z - 2}{1 - 2}$

$\Rightarrow \dfrac{x - 1}{1} = \dfrac{y + 1}{1} = \dfrac{z - 2}{-1} = \lambda$

Any point on this line can be written as $(\lambda + 1, \lambda - 1, -\lambda + 2)$.

The line meets the XY plane at point A, where the z-coordinate is zero:

$-\lambda + 2 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = 2$

Substituting $\lambda = 2$, the coordinates of A are $(3, 1, 0)$.

The line meets the YZ plane at point B, where the x-coordinate is zero:

$\lambda + 1 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = -1$

Substituting $\lambda = -1$, the coordinates of B are $(0, -2, 3)$.

The distance between points A and B is:

$AB = \sqrt{(3 - 0)^2 + (1 - (-2))^2 + (0 - 3)^2}$

$AB = \sqrt{9 + 9 + 9} = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3}$

Answer: $3\sqrt{3}$

</details>

---

### Q24. MHT CET 2026 (19 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $p$ is the shortest distance between the lines $\dfrac{x+1}{7} = \dfrac{y+1}{-6} = z+1$ and $\vec{r} = (3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 7\hat{k}) + \mu(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$ then $[p]$ is... ,(where $[\,.\,]$ denotes the greatest integer function.)

**Options:**

- **(A)** $5$
- **(B)** $20$
- **(C)** $10$
- **(D)** $8$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $10$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The shortest distance $p$ between two lines $\vec{r} = \vec{a}_1 + \lambda \vec{b}_1$ and $\vec{r} = \vec{a}_2 + \mu \vec{b}_2$ is given by $p = \dfrac{|(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)|}{|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2|}$.

For the first line, $\vec{a}_1 = -\hat{i} - \hat{j} - \hat{k}$ and $\vec{b}_1 = 7\hat{i} - 6\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

For the second line, $\vec{a}_2 = 3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 7\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b}_2 = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

$\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1 = 4\hat{i} + 6\hat{j} + 8\hat{k}$

$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 7 & -6 & 1 \\ 1 & -2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = -4\hat{i} - 6\hat{j} - 8\hat{k}$

$(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2) = (4)(-4) + (6)(-6) + (8)(-8) = -16 - 36 - 64 = -116$

$|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2| = \sqrt{(-4)^2 + (-6)^2 + (-8)^2} = \sqrt{16 + 36 + 64} = \sqrt{116}$

$p = \dfrac{|-116|}{\sqrt{116}} = \sqrt{116}$

Since $100 < 116 < 121$, we have $10 < \sqrt{116} < 11$.

Therefore, $[p] = 10$.

Answer: $10$

</details>

---

### Q25. MHT CET 2026 (19 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The symmetric form of the equation of the line $x = ay + b$, $z = cy + d$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{x - a}{b} = y = \dfrac{z - c}{d}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{x - b}{a} = \dfrac{y - d}{c} = z$
- **(C)** $x = \dfrac{y - a}{b} = \dfrac{z - c}{d}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{x - b}{a} = y = \dfrac{z - d}{c}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\dfrac{x - b}{a} = y = \dfrac{z - d}{c}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given equations of the line are $x = ay + b$ and $z = cy + d$.

From the first equation, expressing $y$ in terms of $x$:

$x - b = ay \Rightarrow y = \dfrac{x - b}{a}$

From the second equation, expressing $y$ in terms of $z$:

$z - d = cy \Rightarrow y = \dfrac{z - d}{c}$

Equating the expressions for $y$, the symmetric form of the line is:

$\dfrac{x - b}{a} = y = \dfrac{z - d}{c}$

Answer: $\dfrac{x - b}{a} = y = \dfrac{z - d}{c}$

</details>

---

### Q26. MHT CET 2026 (18 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The acute angle between the lines $2x = 3y = -z$ and $6x = -y = -4z$ is $\ldots$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{\pi}{4}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{\pi}{3}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{\pi}{6}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{\pi}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\dfrac{\pi}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equations of the given lines can be rewritten in symmetric form as:

$L_1: \dfrac{x}{\dfrac{1}{2}} = \dfrac{y}{\dfrac{1}{3}} = \dfrac{z}{-1} \Rightarrow \dfrac{x}{3} = \dfrac{y}{2} = \dfrac{z}{-6}$

$L_2: \dfrac{x}{\dfrac{1}{6}} = \dfrac{y}{-1} = \dfrac{z}{-\dfrac{1}{4}} \Rightarrow \dfrac{x}{2} = \dfrac{y}{-12} = \dfrac{z}{-3}$

The direction ratios of the lines are proportional to the vectors $\vec{d}_1 = 3\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 6\hat{k}$ and $\vec{d}_2 = 2\hat{i} - 12\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}$.

The angle $\theta$ between the lines is given by $\cos \theta = \dfrac{|\vec{d}_1 \cdot \vec{d}_2|}{|\vec{d}_1| |\vec{d}_2|}$.

Calculating the dot product:

$\vec{d}_1 \cdot \vec{d}_2 = (3)(2) + (2)(-12) + (-6)(-3) = 6 - 24 + 18 = 0$

Since the dot product is zero, the lines are perpendicular. Thus, the acute angle between them is $\dfrac{\pi}{2}$.

Answer: $\dfrac{\pi}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q27. MHT CET 2026 (18 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The line $\ell$ passes through the point $(2, 1, 1)$ and is parallel to the plane $x + y + 2z = 18$. If line $\ell$ intersects the line $\dfrac{x+2}{3} = \dfrac{y+1}{-1} = \dfrac{z-2}{1}$, then equation of the line $\ell$ is...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{x-2}{3} = \dfrac{y-1}{1} = \dfrac{z-1}{-2}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-2}{3} = \dfrac{z-2}{-2}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{x-2}{3} = \dfrac{y-1}{-1} = \dfrac{z-1}{-1}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-4}{3} = \dfrac{z+1}{-2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-4}{3} = \dfrac{z+1}{-2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the point of intersection of line $\ell$ and the given line be $Q$.

Any point on the given line $\dfrac{x+2}{3} = \dfrac{y+1}{-1} = \dfrac{z-2}{1} = \lambda$ can be written as $Q(3\lambda - 2, -\lambda - 1, \lambda + 2)$.

The line $\ell$ passes through $P(2, 1, 1)$ and $Q$. The direction ratios of $\ell$ are proportional to the vector $\overrightarrow{PQ}$:

$\overrightarrow{PQ} = (3\lambda - 2 - 2)\hat{i} + (-\lambda - 1 - 1)\hat{j} + (\lambda + 2 - 1)\hat{k}$

$\overrightarrow{PQ} = (3\lambda - 4)\hat{i} + (-\lambda - 2)\hat{j} + (\lambda + 1)\hat{k}$

Since the line $\ell$ is parallel to the plane $x + y + 2z = 18$, it is perpendicular to the normal vector of the plane, $\vec{n} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

Therefore, $\overrightarrow{PQ} \cdot \vec{n} = 0$:

$1(3\lambda - 4) + 1(-\lambda - 2) + 2(\lambda + 1) = 0$

$3\lambda - 4 - \lambda - 2 + 2\lambda + 2 = 0$

$4\lambda - 4 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$

Substituting $\lambda = 1$, the direction ratios of $\ell$ are $(-1, -3, 2)$, which are proportional to $(1, 3, -2)$.

The equation of the line $\ell$ passing through $P(2, 1, 1)$ with direction ratios $(1, 3, -2)$ is:

$\dfrac{x-2}{1} = \dfrac{y-1}{3} = \dfrac{z-1}{-2}$

To match the given options, we can find another point on this line by equating the ratios to $1$:

$x - 2 = 1 \Rightarrow x = 3$

$y - 1 = 3 \Rightarrow y = 4$

$z - 1 = -2 \Rightarrow z = -1$

Thus, the point $(3, 4, -1)$ lies on the line $\ell$. The equation of the line can be rewritten as:

$\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-4}{3} = \dfrac{z+1}{-2}$

Answer: $\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-4}{3} = \dfrac{z+1}{-2}$

</details>

---

### Q28. MHT CET 2026 (18 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The coordinates of the point of intersection of the lines $\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-5}{2} = \dfrac{z-1}{-1}$ and $\dfrac{x-4}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-1} = \dfrac{z-4}{2}$ are...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(2, 3, 2)$
- **(B)** $(-2, 3, -2)$
- **(C)** $(-2, -3, 2)$
- **(D)** $(2, 3, -2)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $(2, 3, 2)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given lines be $L_1: \dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-5}{2} = \dfrac{z-1}{-1} = \lambda$ and $L_2: \dfrac{x-4}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-1} = \dfrac{z-4}{2} = \mu$.

Any point on $L_1$ is given by $(\lambda + 3, 2\lambda + 5, -\lambda + 1)$.

Any point on $L_2$ is given by $(2\mu + 4, -\mu + 2, 2\mu + 4)$.

At the point of intersection, the coordinates must be equal:

$\lambda + 3 = 2\mu + 4 \Rightarrow \lambda - 2\mu = 1$

$2\lambda + 5 = -\mu + 2 \Rightarrow 2\lambda + \mu = -3$

$-\lambda + 1 = 2\mu + 4 \Rightarrow \lambda + 2\mu = -3$

Solving the first and third equations:

$(\lambda - 2\mu) + (\lambda + 2\mu) = 1 - 3 \Rightarrow 2\lambda = -2 \Rightarrow \lambda = -1$

Substituting $\lambda = -1$ in the first equation:

$-1 - 2\mu = 1 \Rightarrow 2\mu = -2 \Rightarrow \mu = -1$

These values satisfy the second equation $2(-1) + (-1) = -3$.

Substituting $\lambda = -1$ into the coordinates of the point on $L_1$:

$x = -1 + 3 = 2$

$y = 2(-1) + 5 = 3$

$z = -(-1) + 1 = 2$

The point of intersection is $(2, 3, 2)$.

Answer: $(2, 3, 2)$

</details>

---

### Q29. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The equation of the perpendicular line from the point $(2,-3,1)$ to the line $\dfrac{x + 1}{2} = \dfrac{y - 3}{3} = \dfrac{z + 2}{-1}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{x - 2}{24} = \dfrac{y + 3}{13} = \dfrac{z - 1}{9}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{x - 2}{24} = \dfrac{y - 3}{-13} = \dfrac{z - 1}{9}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{x + 2}{-24} = \dfrac{y + 3}{13} = \dfrac{z + 1}{-9}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{x - 2}{-24} = \dfrac{y + 3}{13} = \dfrac{z - 1}{-9}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\dfrac{x - 2}{-24} = \dfrac{y + 3}{13} = \dfrac{z - 1}{-9}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given point be $P(2, -3, 1)$ and the given line be $L: \dfrac{x + 1}{2} = \dfrac{y - 3}{3} = \dfrac{z + 2}{-1} = \lambda$.

Any point on the line $L$ can be written as $Q(2\lambda - 1, 3\lambda + 3, -\lambda - 2)$.

Let $Q$ be the foot of the perpendicular from $P$ to the line $L$. The direction ratios of the line segment $PQ$ are given by the difference of the coordinates of $Q$ and $P$:

$a = 2\lambda - 1 - 2 = 2\lambda - 3$

$b = 3\lambda + 3 - (-3) = 3\lambda + 6$

$c = -\lambda - 2 - 1 = -\lambda - 3$

Since $PQ$ is perpendicular to the line $L$, the dot product of their direction ratios must be zero. The direction ratios of $L$ are $2, 3, -1$.

$2(2\lambda - 3) + 3(3\lambda + 6) - 1(-\lambda - 3) = 0$

$4\lambda - 6 + 9\lambda + 18 + \lambda + 3 = 0$

$14\lambda + 15 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = -\dfrac{15}{14}$

Substituting the value of $\lambda$ back into the direction ratios of $PQ$, we get:

$a = 2\left(-\dfrac{15}{14}\right) - 3 = -\dfrac{72}{14}$

$b = 3\left(-\dfrac{15}{14}\right) + 6 = \dfrac{39}{14}$

$c = -\left(-\dfrac{15}{14}\right) - 3 = -\dfrac{27}{14}$

The direction ratios of $PQ$ are proportional to $-72, 39, -27$. Dividing by $3$, the direction ratios can be written as $-24, 13, -9$.

The equation of the perpendicular line passing through $P(2, -3, 1)$ with direction ratios $-24, 13, -9$ is:

$\dfrac{x - 2}{-24} = \dfrac{y + 3}{13} = \dfrac{z - 1}{-9}$

Answer: $\dfrac{x - 2}{-24} = \dfrac{y + 3}{13} = \dfrac{z - 1}{-9}$

</details>

---

### Q30. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The angle between the line $x - 1 = 2 - y = \dfrac{2z - 6}{4}$ and the plane $\vec{r} \cdot (2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}) = 10$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{\pi}{3}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{\pi}{6}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{\pi}{4}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{\pi}{12}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\dfrac{\pi}{6}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the line can be rewritten in standard form as:

$\dfrac{x - 1}{1} = \dfrac{y - 2}{-1} = \dfrac{z - 3}{2}$

The direction vector of the line is $\vec{b} = \hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

The equation of the plane is $\vec{r} \cdot (2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}) = 10$.

The normal vector to the plane is $\vec{n} = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

The angle $\theta$ between the line and the plane is given by:

$\sin \theta = \dfrac{|\vec{b} \cdot \vec{n}|}{|\vec{b}| |\vec{n}|}$

$\sin \theta = \dfrac{|(1)(2) + (-1)(1) + (2)(1)|}{\sqrt{1^2 + (-1)^2 + 2^2} \sqrt{2^2 + 1^2 + 1^2}}$

$\sin \theta = \dfrac{|2 - 1 + 2|}{\sqrt{6} \sqrt{6}} = \dfrac{3}{6} = \dfrac{1}{2}$

$\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{6}$

Answer: $\dfrac{\pi}{6}$

</details>

---

### Q31. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The sum of the coordinates of one of the points on the line $\dfrac{x - 2}{1} = \dfrac{y + 3}{-2} = \dfrac{z + 5}{2}$ which is at a distance of 3 units from the point $(2, -3, -5)$ is ...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $7$
- **(B)** $-7$
- **(C)** $11$
- **(D)** $-11$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $-7$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let any point on the given line $\dfrac{x - 2}{1} = \dfrac{y + 3}{-2} = \dfrac{z + 5}{2} = \lambda$ be $(2 + \lambda, -3 - 2\lambda, -5 + 2\lambda)$.

The distance of this point from $(2, -3, -5)$ is given as $3$ units.

Using the distance formula:

$\sqrt{(2 + \lambda - 2)^2 + (-3 - 2\lambda + 3)^2 + (-5 + 2\lambda + 5)^2} = 3$

$\sqrt{\lambda^2 + (-2\lambda)^2 + (2\lambda)^2} = 3$

$\sqrt{\lambda^2 + 4\lambda^2 + 4\lambda^2} = 3$

$\sqrt{9\lambda^2} = 3$

$3|\lambda| = 3 \Rightarrow \lambda = \pm 1$

For $\lambda = 1$, the coordinates of the point are $(2 + 1, -3 - 2(1), -5 + 2(1)) = (3, -5, -3)$.
The sum of the coordinates is $3 - 5 - 3 = -5$.

For $\lambda = -1$, the coordinates of the point are $(2 - 1, -3 - 2(-1), -5 + 2(-1)) = (1, -1, -7)$.
The sum of the coordinates is $1 - 1 - 7 = -7$.

Since $-7$ is present in the given options, it is the required sum.

Answer: $-7$

</details>

---

### Q32. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the distance of point $B(2,1,-3)$ from the line passing through the point $A(4,-2,2)$, and parallel to the vector $\vec{c} = -4\hat{i} - 6\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$ is $x$, then $x^4 + x^2 + 541 =$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2026$
- **(B)** $2025$
- **(C)** $2024$
- **(D)** $2023$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $2023$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The position vectors of points $A$ and $B$ are $\vec{a} = 4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j} - 3\hat{k}$.

The vector joining $A$ to $B$ is:
$\overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{b} - \vec{a} = (2-4)\hat{i} + (1 - (-2))\hat{j} + (-3-2)\hat{k} = -2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - 5\hat{k}$

The line passes through $A$ and is parallel to the vector $\vec{c} = -4\hat{i} - 6\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$. We can take the direction vector of the line as $\vec{d} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

Taking the dot product of $\overrightarrow{AB}$ and $\vec{d}$:
$\overrightarrow{AB} \cdot \vec{d} = (-2)(2) + (3)(3) + (-5)(1) = -4 + 9 - 5 = 0$

Since $\overrightarrow{AB}$ is perpendicular to the direction of the line, the perpendicular distance $x$ of point $B$ from the line is simply the magnitude of $\overrightarrow{AB}$.

$x = |\overrightarrow{AB}| = \sqrt{(-2)^2 + 3^2 + (-5)^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9 + 25} = \sqrt{38}$

We need to find the value of $x^4 + x^2 + 541$.

$x^2 = 38$

$x^4 = (38)^2 = 1444$

Substituting these values:
$x^4 + x^2 + 541 = 1444 + 38 + 541 = 2023$

Answer: $2023$

</details>

---

### Q33. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If $\theta$ is the angle between the lines $\dfrac{x-1}{2} = \dfrac{2y+3}{4};\ z = -2$ and $x = 1;\ \dfrac{y-1}{2} = \dfrac{z+1}{2}$, then

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{6}$
- **(B)** $\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{3}$
- **(C)** $\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{4}$
- **(D)** $\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the first line can be rewritten as $\dfrac{x-1}{2} = \dfrac{y+\dfrac{3}{2}}{2} = \dfrac{z+2}{0}$

The direction ratios of the first line are $2, 2, 0$.

The equation of the second line can be rewritten as $\dfrac{x-1}{0} = \dfrac{y-1}{2} = \dfrac{z+1}{2}$

The direction ratios of the second line are $0, 2, 2$.

Let $\theta$ be the angle between the two lines.

$\cos\theta = \dfrac{a_1a_2 + b_1b_2 + c_1c_2}{\sqrt{a_1^2+b_1^2+c_1^2}\sqrt{a_2^2+b_2^2+c_2^2}}$

$\cos\theta = \dfrac{2(0) + 2(2) + 0(2)}{\sqrt{2^2+2^2+0^2}\sqrt{0^2+2^2+2^2}}$

$\cos\theta = \dfrac{4}{\sqrt{8}\sqrt{8}} = \dfrac{4}{8} = \dfrac{1}{2}$

$\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{3}$

Answer: $\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{3}$

</details>

---

### Q34. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If for some $m \in \mathbb{R}$ the lines $L_1 : \dfrac{x+1}{m} = \dfrac{y-m}{-1} = \dfrac{z-1}{1}$ and $L_2 : \dfrac{x+2}{-4} = \dfrac{y+1}{9} = \dfrac{z+1}{1}$ are coplanar, then line $L_1$ passes through the point

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(-7, 2, -5)$
- **(B)** $(7, -2, 5)$
- **(C)** $(7, 2, 5)$
- **(D)** $(7, -2, -5)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $(7, -2, 5)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

For the lines $L_1$ and $L_2$ to be coplanar, the shortest distance between them must be zero. The condition for coplanarity of two lines is given by:

$\begin{vmatrix} x_2 - x_1 & y_2 - y_1 & z_2 - z_1 \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

From the given equations, the lines pass through the points $(-1, m, 1)$ and $(-2, -1, -1)$ respectively. Their direction ratios are $(m, -1, 1)$ and $(-4, 9, 1)$.

Substituting these values into the determinant:

$\begin{vmatrix} -2 - (-1) & -1 - m & -1 - 1 \\ m & -1 & 1 \\ -4 & 9 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

$\begin{vmatrix} -1 & -1-m & -2 \\ m & -1 & 1 \\ -4 & 9 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$-1(-1 - 9) - (-1 - m)(m - (-4)) - 2(9m - 4) = 0$

$10 + (1 + m)(m + 4) - 18m + 8 = 0$

$10 + m^2 + 5m + 4 - 18m + 8 = 0$

$m^2 - 13m + 22 = 0$

$(m - 2)(m - 11) = 0$

This gives $m = 2$ or $m = 11$.

For $m = 2$, the equation of line $L_1$ becomes:

$\dfrac{x+1}{2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-1} = \dfrac{z-1}{1}$

Checking the given options by substituting the coordinates into the equation of $L_1$ for $m = 2$:

For the point $(7, -2, 5)$:

$\dfrac{7+1}{2} = \dfrac{8}{2} = 4$

$\dfrac{-2-2}{-1} = \dfrac{-4}{-1} = 4$

$\dfrac{5-1}{1} = \dfrac{4}{1} = 4$

Since all three parts are equal, the line $L_1$ passes through the point $(7, -2, 5)$.

</details>

---

### Q35. MHT CET 2026 (17 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\vec{r} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k} + \lambda(q\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$ and $\vec{r} = p\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k} + \mu(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$ intersect each other and $q\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$ is collinear to $4\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$, then the values of $p$ and $q$ are

**Options:**

- **(A)** $p = 4,\ q = 3$
- **(B)** $p = 2,\ q = 3$
- **(C)** $p = 4,\ q = 2$
- **(D)** $p = 4,\ q = 1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $p = 4,\ q = 2$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Given that the vector $q\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$ is collinear to $4\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$, their direction ratios must be proportional.

$\dfrac{q}{4} = \dfrac{-2}{-4} = \dfrac{1}{2}$

$\Rightarrow q = 2$

The equations of the lines become:

$\vec{r} = \hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k} + \lambda(2\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

$\vec{r} = p\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k} + \mu(\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$

Since the lines intersect, their general points must coincide for some values of $\lambda$ and $\mu$. Equating the respective components, we get:

$1 + 2\lambda = p + \mu$

$1 - 2\lambda = -3 - 2\mu$

$-1 + \lambda = 2 + 2\mu$

From the second equation:

$2\lambda - 2\mu = 4 \Rightarrow \lambda - \mu = 2$

From the third equation:

$\lambda - 2\mu = 3$

Subtracting the third equation from the modified second equation:

$(\lambda - \mu) - (\lambda - 2\mu) = 2 - 3$

$\mu = -1$

Substituting $\mu = -1$ into $\lambda - \mu = 2$:

$\lambda - (-1) = 2 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$

Substituting $\lambda = 1$ and $\mu = -1$ into the first equation:

$1 + 2(1) = p - 1$

$3 = p - 1 \Rightarrow p = 4$

Thus, $p = 4$ and $q = 2$.

Answer: $p = 4,\ q = 2$

</details>

---

### Q36. MHT CET 2026 (16 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If the lines $2x = ky = -z$ and $6x = -y = -4z$ are perpendicular to each other then the value of $k$ is ...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $16$
- **(B)** $5$
- **(C)** $10$
- **(D)** $3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $3$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equations of the given lines can be rewritten in standard symmetric form as:

$\dfrac{x}{1/2} = \dfrac{y}{1/k} = \dfrac{z}{-1}$

$\dfrac{x}{1/6} = \dfrac{y}{-1} = \dfrac{z}{-1/4}$

The direction ratios of the first line are $a_1 = \dfrac{1}{2}$, $b_1 = \dfrac{1}{k}$, $c_1 = -1$.

The direction ratios of the second line are $a_2 = \dfrac{1}{6}$, $b_2 = -1$, $c_2 = -\dfrac{1}{4}$.

Since the two lines are perpendicular to each other, the sum of the products of their corresponding direction ratios must be zero:

$a_1 a_2 + b_1 b_2 + c_1 c_2 = 0$

$\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\left(\dfrac{1}{6}\right) + \left(\dfrac{1}{k}\right)(-1) + (-1)\left(-\dfrac{1}{4}\right) = 0$

$\dfrac{1}{12} - \dfrac{1}{k} + \dfrac{1}{4} = 0$

$\dfrac{1}{k} = \dfrac{1}{12} + \dfrac{3}{12}$

$\dfrac{1}{k} = \dfrac{4}{12}$

$\dfrac{1}{k} = \dfrac{1}{3}$

$k = 3$

Answer: $3$

</details>

---

### Q37. MHT CET 2026 (16 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The lines $\dfrac{x - 2}{1} = \dfrac{y - 3}{1} = \dfrac{z - 4}{-k}$ and $\dfrac{x - 1}{k} = \dfrac{y - 4}{2} = \dfrac{z - 5}{1}$ are coplanar if

**Options:**

- **(A)** $k = 0, -3$
- **(B)** $k = -1, 3$
- **(C)** $k = 1, 2$
- **(D)** $k = 2, 4$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $k = 0, -3$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The condition for two lines $\dfrac{x - x_1}{a_1} = \dfrac{y - y_1}{b_1} = \dfrac{z - z_1}{c_1}$ and $\dfrac{x - x_2}{a_2} = \dfrac{y - y_2}{b_2} = \dfrac{z - z_2}{c_2}$ to be coplanar is:

$\begin{vmatrix} x_2 - x_1 & y_2 - y_1 & z_2 - z_1 \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

From the given equations of the lines, we have $(x_1, y_1, z_1) = (2, 3, 4)$ and $(x_2, y_2, z_2) = (1, 4, 5)$.

The direction ratios are $(a_1, b_1, c_1) = (1, 1, -k)$ and $(a_2, b_2, c_2) = (k, 2, 1)$.

Substituting these values into the determinant condition:

$\begin{vmatrix} 1 - 2 & 4 - 3 & 5 - 4 \\ 1 & 1 & -k \\ k & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

$\begin{vmatrix} -1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & -k \\ k & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$-1(1 \cdot 1 - (-k) \cdot 2) - 1(1 \cdot 1 - (-k) \cdot k) + 1(1 \cdot 2 - 1 \cdot k) = 0$

$-1(1 + 2k) - 1(1 + k^2) + 1(2 - k) = 0$

$-1 - 2k - 1 - k^2 + 2 - k = 0$

$-k^2 - 3k = 0$

$k(k + 3) = 0$

$\Rightarrow k = 0, -3$

Answer: $k = 0, -3$

</details>

---

### Q38. MHT CET 2026 (16 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The lines $\dfrac{x - 1}{-1} = \dfrac{y + 2}{1} = \dfrac{z - 3}{-2}$ and $\dfrac{x - 1}{1} = \dfrac{y + 2}{1} = \dfrac{z + 1}{-2}$ are

**Options:**

- **(A)** intersecting but not perpendicular
- **(B)** parallel
- **(C)** perpendicular
- **(D)** skew lines

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) skew lines**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the given lines are $\vec{b}_1 = -\hat{i} + \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b}_2 = \hat{i} + \hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$.

The lines pass through the points $\vec{a}_1 = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$ and $\vec{a}_2 = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}$.

The vector connecting the points is $\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1 = -4\hat{k}$.

The cross product of the direction vectors is:

$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ -1 & 1 & -2 \\ 1 & 1 & -2 \end{vmatrix} = 0\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$

Now, we find the scalar triple product $(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)$:

$(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2) = (-4\hat{k}) \cdot (-4\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}) = 8 \neq 0$

Since the scalar triple product is non-zero, the shortest distance between the lines is non-zero. Also, the direction vectors are not proportional, meaning the lines are not parallel.

Therefore, the given lines are skew lines.

Answer: skew lines

</details>

---

### Q39. MHT CET 2026 (16 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The vector equation of the line whose cartesian equations are $x = 2, 2y - 3z + 7 = 0$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\vec{r} = (2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k})$
- **(B)** $\vec{r} = (2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{i} - 3\hat{j})$
- **(C)** $\vec{r} = \left(2\hat{i} + \dfrac{7}{3}\hat{k}\right) + \lambda(3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$
- **(D)** $\vec{r} = \left(-2\hat{i} + \dfrac{7}{3}\hat{k}\right) + \lambda(3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\vec{r} = \left(2\hat{i} + \dfrac{7}{3}\hat{k}\right) + \lambda(3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given cartesian equations of the line are $x = 2$ and $2y - 3z + 7 = 0$.

We can rewrite these equations in symmetric form to find a point on the line and its direction ratios.

$x = 2 \Rightarrow x - 2 = 0 \Rightarrow \dfrac{x - 2}{0}$

$2y - 3z + 7 = 0 \Rightarrow 2y = 3z - 7 \Rightarrow \dfrac{y - 0}{3} = \dfrac{z - \dfrac{7}{3}}{2}$

Equating them, we get the symmetric form of the line:

$\dfrac{x - 2}{0} = \dfrac{y - 0}{3} = \dfrac{z - \dfrac{7}{3}}{2}$

This shows that the line passes through the point with position vector $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + \dfrac{7}{3}\hat{k}$ and is parallel to the vector $\vec{b} = 0\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

The vector equation of the line is given by $\vec{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda \vec{b}$.

Substituting the values of $\vec{a}$ and $\vec{b}$, we get:

$\vec{r} = \left(2\hat{i} + \dfrac{7}{3}\hat{k}\right) + \lambda(3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$

Answer: $\vec{r} = \left(2\hat{i} + \dfrac{7}{3}\hat{k}\right) + \lambda(3\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$

</details>

---

### Q40. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The co-ordinates of the point where the line joining the points $(3,5,-7)$ and $(-2, 1, 8)$ is intersected by the YOZ plane are

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\left(0, \dfrac{-13}{5}, \dfrac{2}{5}\right)$
- **(B)** $\left(0, \dfrac{13}{5}, \dfrac{2}{5}\right)$
- **(C)** $\left(0, \dfrac{13}{5}, 2\right)$
- **(D)** $\left(0, \dfrac{-13}{5}, -2\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\left(0, \dfrac{13}{5}, 2\right)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the line joining the points $(3, 5, -7)$ and $(-2, 1, 8)$ is given by

$\dfrac{x - 3}{-2 - 3} = \dfrac{y - 5}{1 - 5} = \dfrac{z - (-7)}{8 - (-7)}$

$\dfrac{x - 3}{-5} = \dfrac{y - 5}{-4} = \dfrac{z + 7}{15} = k$

Any point on this line is of the form $(-5k + 3, -4k + 5, 15k - 7)$.

The line intersects the YOZ plane, where the x-coordinate is zero.

$-5k + 3 = 0 \Rightarrow k = \dfrac{3}{5}$

Substituting $k = \dfrac{3}{5}$ in the y and z coordinates, we get

$y = -4\left(\dfrac{3}{5}\right) + 5 = \dfrac{-12 + 25}{5} = \dfrac{13}{5}$

$z = 15\left(\dfrac{3}{5}\right) - 7 = 9 - 7 = 2$

The required coordinates are $\left(0, \dfrac{13}{5}, 2\right)$.

Answer: $\left(0, \dfrac{13}{5}, 2\right)$

</details>

---

### Q41. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\vec{r} = (\hat{i} + m\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 4\hat{k})$ and $\vec{r} = (4\hat{i} + \hat{j}) + \mu(5\hat{i} + m\hat{j} + \hat{k})$ intersect each other, then m =

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1$
- **(B)** $-1$
- **(C)** $2$
- **(D)** $-2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $2$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Equating the position vectors of the two lines for intersection:

$1 + 2\lambda = 4 + 5\mu$

$m + 3\lambda = 1 + m\mu$

$3 + 4\lambda = \mu$

From the third equation, $\mu = 4\lambda + 3$.

Substituting this into the first equation:

$2\lambda - 5(4\lambda + 3) = 3$

$-18\lambda - 15 = 3$

$-18\lambda = 18 \Rightarrow \lambda = -1$

Substituting $\lambda = -1$ into $\mu = 4\lambda + 3$ gives $\mu = -1$.

Now, substituting $\lambda = -1$ and $\mu = -1$ into the second equation:

$m + 3(-1) = 1 + m(-1)$

$m - 3 = 1 - m$

$2m = 4 \Rightarrow m = 2$

Answer: $2$

</details>

---

### Q42. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $x = -1 + s$, $y = 3 - \lambda s$, $z = 1 + \lambda s$ and $x = \dfrac{t}{2}$, $y = 1 + t$, $z = 2 - t$ with parameters s and t, are coplanar, then $\lambda =$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2$
- **(B)** $1$
- **(C)** $-2$
- **(D)** $-1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $-2$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equations of the given lines can be written in symmetric form as:

Line 1: $\dfrac{x - (-1)}{1} = \dfrac{y - 3}{-\lambda} = \dfrac{z - 1}{\lambda} = s$
This line passes through the point $A(-1, 3, 1)$ and has direction ratios $\vec{b_1} = \langle 1, -\lambda, \lambda \rangle$.

Line 2: $\dfrac{x - 0}{\dfrac{1}{2}} = \dfrac{y - 1}{1} = \dfrac{z - 2}{-1} = t$
This line passes through the point $B(0, 1, 2)$ and has direction ratios $\vec{b_2} = \langle \dfrac{1}{2}, 1, -1 \rangle$.

The vector joining the points $A$ and $B$ is:
$\overrightarrow{AB} = (0 - (-1))\hat{i} + (1 - 3)\hat{j} + (2 - 1)\hat{k} = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$

For the two lines to be coplanar, the vectors $\overrightarrow{AB}$, $\vec{b_1}$, and $\vec{b_2}$ must be coplanar. Thus, their scalar triple product must be zero:

$\begin{vmatrix} 1 & -2 & 1 \\ 1 & -\lambda & \lambda \\ \dfrac{1}{2} & 1 & -1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:
$1\left((-\lambda)(-1) - (\lambda)(1)\right) - (-2)\left(1(-1) - \lambda\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\right) + 1\left(1(1) - (-\lambda)\left(\dfrac{1}{2}\right)\right) = 0$

$1(\lambda - \lambda) + 2\left(-1 - \dfrac{\lambda}{2}\right) + 1\left(1 + \dfrac{\lambda}{2}\right) = 0$

$0 - 2 - \lambda + 1 + \dfrac{\lambda}{2} = 0$

$-1 - \dfrac{\lambda}{2} = 0$

$\dfrac{\lambda}{2} = -1 \Rightarrow \lambda = -2$

Answer: $-2$

</details>

---

### Q43. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If the lines $\dfrac{x-5}{5m+2} = \dfrac{2-y}{5} = \dfrac{1-z}{-1}$ and $x = \dfrac{2y+1}{4m} = \dfrac{1-z}{-3}$ are perpendicular to each other, then the value of m is ...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1$
- **(B)** $0$
- **(C)** $2$
- **(D)** $-1$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Rewriting the equations of the given lines in standard form.

First line: $\dfrac{x-5}{5m+2} = \dfrac{y-2}{-5} = \dfrac{z-1}{1}$

Direction ratios of the first line are $a_1 = 5m+2$, $b_1 = -5$, $c_1 = 1$.

Second line: $\dfrac{x-0}{1} = \dfrac{y+\dfrac{1}{2}}{2m} = \dfrac{z-1}{3}$

Direction ratios of the second line are $a_2 = 1$, $b_2 = 2m$, $c_2 = 3$.

Since the lines are perpendicular, $a_1 a_2 + b_1 b_2 + c_1 c_2 = 0$.

$(5m+2)(1) + (-5)(2m) + (1)(3) = 0$

$5m + 2 - 10m + 3 = 0$

$-5m + 5 = 0$

$m = 1$

Answer: $1$

</details>

### Q44. MHT CET 2026 (15 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

For the line $\dfrac{x+1}{1} = \dfrac{y-2}{2} = \dfrac{z+3}{3}$, identify the incorrect statement among the following.

**Options:**

- **(A)** It can be represented by equation $\dfrac{x+2}{1} = \dfrac{y}{2} = \dfrac{z+6}{3}$
- **(B)** It lies in the plane $x - 2y + z + 8 = 0$
- **(C)** It is perpendicular to the plane $x - 2y + z = 0$
- **(D)** It passes through point $(0, 4, 0)$.

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) It is perpendicular to the plane $x - 2y + z = 0$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given line is $\dfrac{x+1}{1} = \dfrac{y-2}{2} = \dfrac{z+3}{3} = \lambda$.

Any point on the line is of the form $(\lambda - 1, 2\lambda + 2, 3\lambda - 3)$.

For $\lambda = -1$, the point is $(-2, 0, -6)$. Since the direction ratios remain the same, the line can be represented as $\dfrac{x+2}{1} = \dfrac{y}{2} = \dfrac{z+6}{3}$. Thus, the first statement is correct.

Substituting the general point into the plane $x - 2y + z + 8 = 0$, we get $(\lambda - 1) - 2(2\lambda + 2) + (3\lambda - 3) + 8 = \lambda - 1 - 4\lambda - 4 + 3\lambda - 3 + 8 = 0$, which is true for all $\lambda$. Thus, the line lies in the plane, making the second statement correct.

For $\lambda = 1$, the point is $(0, 4, 0)$. Thus, the line passes through $(0, 4, 0)$, making the fourth statement correct.

The direction ratios of the line are $(1, 2, 3)$ and the normal to the plane $x - 2y + z = 0$ has direction ratios $(1, -2, 1)$. For the line to be perpendicular to the plane, their direction ratios must be proportional. Since $\dfrac{1}{1} \neq \dfrac{2}{-2} \neq \dfrac{3}{1}$, the line is not perpendicular to the plane. Thus, the third statement is incorrect.

Answer: It is perpendicular to the plane $x - 2y + z = 0$

</details>

---

### Q45. MHT CET 2026 (13 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The equation of a line passing through a point $(4, -2, 3)$ and perpendicular to the XZ-plane is....

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\bar{r} = (4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} + \hat{k})$
- **(B)** $\bar{r} = (4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i})$
- **(C)** $\bar{r} = (4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{j})$
- **(D)** $\bar{r} = (4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} - \hat{k})$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\bar{r} = (4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{j})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The position vector of the given point is $\vec{a} = 4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$.

Since the line is perpendicular to the XZ-plane, it is parallel to the Y-axis. Therefore, the direction vector of the line is $\vec{b} = \hat{j}$.

The vector equation of a line passing through a point with position vector $\vec{a}$ and parallel to a vector $\vec{b}$ is given by $\vec{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda \vec{b}$.

Substituting the values, we get $\vec{r} = (4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda \hat{j}$.

Answer: $\bar{r} = (4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{j})$

</details>

### Q46. MHT CET 2026 (13 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The point having position vector $4\hat{i} - 11\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$ lies on the line

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\bar{r} = (6\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}) + \lambda(\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 3\hat{k})$
- **(B)** $\bar{r} = (6\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + 3\hat{k})$
- **(C)** $\bar{r} = (6\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + \hat{k})$
- **(D)** $\bar{r} = (6\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 3\hat{k})$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\bar{r} = (6\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 3\hat{k})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the position vector of the given point be $\vec{p} = 4\hat{i} - 11\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

All the given lines pass through the point with position vector $\vec{a} = 6\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}$.

For the point to lie on the line $\vec{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda \vec{b}$, the vector $\vec{p} - \vec{a}$ must be a scalar multiple of the direction vector $\vec{b}$.

Calculating $\vec{p} - \vec{a}$:

$\vec{p} - \vec{a} = (4\hat{i} - 11\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) - (6\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 5\hat{k})$

$\vec{p} - \vec{a} = -2\hat{i} - 7\hat{j} - 3\hat{k} = -1(2\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 3\hat{k})$

This shows that $\vec{p} - \vec{a}$ is a scalar multiple of $2\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$.

Thus, the point lies on the line $\vec{r} = (6\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}) + \lambda(2\hat{i} + 7\hat{j} + 3\hat{k})$ with $\lambda = -1$.

</details>

---

### Q47. MHT CET 2026 (13 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The shortest distance between the lines, where the first line passes through $(0,0,0)$ and $(2,0,3)$ and the second line passes through $(2,5,0)$ and $(0,4,0)$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{24}{7}$ units
- **(B)** $\dfrac{9}{7}$ units
- **(C)** $\dfrac{1}{7}$ units
- **(D)** $\dfrac{36}{7}$ units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\dfrac{24}{7}$ units**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the first line passing through $(0,0,0)$ and $(2,0,3)$ is $\vec{r} = \vec{a_1} + \lambda \vec{b_1}$, where $\vec{a_1} = 0\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b_1} = 2\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$.

The equation of the second line passing through $(2,5,0)$ and $(0,4,0)$ is $\vec{r} = \vec{a_2} + \mu \vec{b_2}$, where $\vec{a_2} = 0\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b_2} = -2\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$.

The shortest distance $d$ between two lines is given by $d = \dfrac{|(\vec{a_2} - \vec{a_1}) \cdot (\vec{b_1} \times \vec{b_2})|}{|\vec{b_1} \times \vec{b_2}|}$.

We have $\vec{a_2} - \vec{a_1} = 4\hat{j}$.

Now, $\vec{b_1} \times \vec{b_2} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 2 & 0 & 3 \\ -2 & -1 & 0 \end{vmatrix} = 3\hat{i} - 6\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$.

The magnitude is $|\vec{b_1} \times \vec{b_2}| = \sqrt{3^2 + (-6)^2 + (-2)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 36 + 4} = 7$.

The dot product is $(\vec{a_2} - \vec{a_1}) \cdot (\vec{b_1} \times \vec{b_2}) = (4\hat{j}) \cdot (3\hat{i} - 6\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}) = -24$.

Therefore, the shortest distance is $d = \dfrac{|-24|}{7} = \dfrac{24}{7}$.

Answer: $\dfrac{24}{7}$ units

</details>

---

### Q48. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The point of intersection of the two lines $\dfrac{x-3}{3} = \dfrac{y-3}{-1}, z - 1 = 0$ and $\dfrac{x-6}{2} = \dfrac{z-1}{3}, y - 2 = 0$ is....

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(0, 0, 0)$
- **(B)** $(1, 2, 6)$
- **(C)** $(3, -1, 0)$
- **(D)** $(6, 2, 1)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $(6, 2, 1)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equations of the first line can be written as $\dfrac{x-3}{3} = \dfrac{y-3}{-1} = t$ and $z = 1$.

Any point on the first line is of the form $(3t+3, -t+3, 1)$.

The equations of the second line can be written as $\dfrac{x-6}{2} = \dfrac{z-1}{3} = s$ and $y = 2$.

Any point on the second line is of the form $(2s+6, 2, 3s+1)$.

At the point of intersection, the coordinates must be equal.

Equating the $y$-coordinates, we get $-t+3 = 2 \Rightarrow t = 1$.

Equating the $z$-coordinates, we get $1 = 3s+1 \Rightarrow s = 0$.

Substituting $t = 1$ in the coordinates of the first line, we get the point $(6, 2, 1)$.

Substituting $s = 0$ in the coordinates of the second line, we also get the point $(6, 2, 1)$.

Thus, the point of intersection is $(6, 2, 1)$.

Answer: $(6, 2, 1)$

</details>

---

### Q49. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\dfrac{x-1}{2} = \dfrac{y+1}{3} = \dfrac{z-1}{4}$ and $\dfrac{x-3}{1} = \dfrac{y-c}{2} = \dfrac{z}{1}$ intersect, then the radius of circle $x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 10y + c = 0$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{9}{\sqrt{2}}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{9}{2}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{7}{\sqrt{2}}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{7}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\dfrac{7}{\sqrt{2}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The condition for the two lines to intersect is that they must be coplanar.

$\begin{vmatrix} 3-1 & c-(-1) & 0-1 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

$\begin{vmatrix} 2 & c+1 & -1 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$2(3 - 8) - (c+1)(2 - 4) - 1(4 - 3) = 0$

$-10 + 2(c+1) - 1 = 0$

$2c - 9 = 0 \Rightarrow c = \dfrac{9}{2}$

The equation of the circle is $x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 10y + \dfrac{9}{2} = 0$.

The radius of the circle is given by $r = \sqrt{g^2 + f^2 - c}$.

$r = \sqrt{(-2)^2 + (5)^2 - \dfrac{9}{2}}$

$r = \sqrt{4 + 25 - \dfrac{9}{2}}$

$r = \sqrt{29 - \dfrac{9}{2}} = \sqrt{\dfrac{49}{2}} = \dfrac{7}{\sqrt{2}}$

Answer: $\dfrac{7}{\sqrt{2}}$

</details>

---

### Q50. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\dfrac{x-1}{2} = \dfrac{y+1}{3} = \dfrac{z-1}{4}$ and $\dfrac{x-2}{1} = \dfrac{y+m}{2} = \dfrac{z-2}{1}$ intersect each other, then the value of $m$ is....

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1$
- **(B)** $2$
- **(C)** $-1$
- **(D)** $4$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $-1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given lines be $L_1$ and $L_2$.

Any point on $L_1$ is given by $(2r_1 + 1, 3r_1 - 1, 4r_1 + 1)$.

Any point on $L_2$ is given by $(r_2 + 2, 2r_2 - m, r_2 + 2)$.

For the lines to intersect, these points must coincide for some values of $r_1$ and $r_2$. Equating the $x$, $y$, and $z$ coordinates:

$2r_1 + 1 = r_2 + 2 \Rightarrow 2r_1 - r_2 = 1$

$3r_1 - 1 = 2r_2 - m \Rightarrow 3r_1 - 2r_2 = 1 - m$

$4r_1 + 1 = r_2 + 2 \Rightarrow 4r_1 - r_2 = 1$

Solving the first and third equations:

$4r_1 - r_2 = 1$

$2r_1 - r_2 = 1$

Subtracting the two equations gives $2r_1 = 0 \Rightarrow r_1 = 0$.

Substituting $r_1 = 0$ in $2r_1 - r_2 = 1$ gives $r_2 = -1$.

Substituting $r_1 = 0$ and $r_2 = -1$ in the second equation:

$3(0) - 2(-1) = 1 - m$

$2 = 1 - m$

$m = -1$

Answer: $-1$

</details>

---

### Q51. MHT CET 2026 (11 April Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $L_1: \dfrac{x-1}{-3} = \dfrac{y-2}{2k} = \dfrac{z-3}{2}, L_2: \dfrac{x-1}{3k} = \dfrac{y-5}{1} = \dfrac{z-6}{-5}$ are perpendicular to each other, then the equation of a plane containing the line $L_1$ and parallel to the line $L_2$ for the value of $k$ that satisfies this condition is ...

**Options:**

- **(A)** $31x + 21y + 46z - 211 = 0$
- **(B)** $602x - 1155y - 747z + 3949 = 0$
- **(C)** $43x + 105y - 154z + 209 = 0$
- **(D)** $2x - 3y + 5z - 11 = 0$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $602x - 1155y - 747z + 3949 = 0$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the line $L_1$ are $\vec{d_1} = \langle -3, 2k, 2 \rangle$.

The direction ratios of the line $L_2$ are $\vec{d_2} = \langle 3k, 1, -5 \rangle$.

Since the lines $L_1$ and $L_2$ are perpendicular, their dot product is zero:

$\vec{d_1} \cdot \vec{d_2} = 0$

$(-3)(3k) + (2k)(1) + (2)(-5) = 0$

$-9k + 2k - 10 = 0 \Rightarrow -7k = 10 \Rightarrow k = -\dfrac{10}{7}$
