Substituting $k = -\dfrac{10}{7}$ into the direction ratios and scaling by $7$ to avoid fractions, we get:

$\vec{v_1} = 7\vec{d_1} = \langle -21, -20, 14 \rangle$

$\vec{v_2} = 7\vec{d_2} = \langle -30, 7, -35 \rangle$

The plane contains $L_1$ and is parallel to $L_2$, so its normal vector $\vec{n}$ is perpendicular to both $\vec{v_1}$ and $\vec{v_2}$:

$\vec{n} = \vec{v_1} \times \vec{v_2} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ -21 & -20 & 14 \\ -30 & 7 & -35 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{n} = \hat{i}(700 - 98) - \hat{j}(735 + 420) + \hat{k}(-147 - 600)$

$\vec{n} = \langle 602, -1155, -747 \rangle$

The plane contains the line $L_1$, so it passes through the point $(1, 2, 3)$ which lies on $L_1$. The equation of the plane is:

$602(x - 1) - 1155(y - 2) - 747(z - 3) = 0$

$602x - 602 - 1155y + 2310 - 747z + 2241 = 0$

$602x - 1155y - 747z + 3949 = 0$

Answer: $602x - 1155y - 747z + 3949 = 0$

</details>

---

### Q52. MHT CET 2025 (5 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

$A$ line $L$ is passing through points $A(1,3,2)$ and $B(2,2,1)$. If mirror image of point $\mathrm{P}(1,1,-1)$ in the line L is $(\mathrm{x}, \mathrm{y}, \mathrm{z})$ then $x+\mathrm{y}+\mathrm{z}=$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{10}{3}$
- **(B)** $\frac{13}{3}$
- **(C)** $\frac{14}{3}$
- **(D)** $\frac{23}{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\frac{23}{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the line $L$ passing through $A(1,3,2)$ and $B(2,2,1)$ are proportional to $(2-1, 2-3, 1-2) = (1, -1, -1)$.

The equation of the line $L$ is:
$\dfrac{X - 1}{1} = \dfrac{Y - 3}{-1} = \dfrac{Z - 2}{-1} = \lambda$

Let $M$ be the foot of the perpendicular from $P(1,1,-1)$ to the line $L$. The coordinates of $M$ can be written as $(\lambda + 1, -\lambda + 3, -\lambda + 2)$.

The direction ratios of the line segment $PM$ are:
$(\lambda + 1 - 1, -\lambda + 3 - 1, -\lambda + 2 - (-1)) = (\lambda, -\lambda + 2, -\lambda + 3)$

Since $PM$ is perpendicular to the line $L$, the dot product of their direction ratios is zero:
$1(\lambda) + (-1)(-\lambda + 2) + (-1)(-\lambda + 3) = 0$

$\lambda + \lambda - 2 + \lambda - 3 = 0$

$3\lambda - 5 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = \dfrac{5}{3}$

Substituting $\lambda = \dfrac{5}{3}$, the coordinates of $M$ are:
$M = \left( \dfrac{5}{3} + 1, -\dfrac{5}{3} + 3, -\dfrac{5}{3} + 2 \right) = \left( \dfrac{8}{3}, \dfrac{4}{3}, \dfrac{1}{3} \right)$

Let the mirror image of $P(1,1,-1)$ in the line $L$ be $P'(x,y,z)$. Since $M$ is the midpoint of $PP'$:
$\dfrac{x + 1}{2} = \dfrac{8}{3} \Rightarrow x = \dfrac{13}{3}$

$\dfrac{y + 1}{2} = \dfrac{4}{3} \Rightarrow y = \dfrac{5}{3}$

$\dfrac{z - 1}{2} = \dfrac{1}{3} \Rightarrow z = \dfrac{5}{3}$

Therefore, $x + y + z = \dfrac{13}{3} + \dfrac{5}{3} + \dfrac{5}{3} = \dfrac{23}{3}$.

Answer: $\frac{23}{3}$

</details>

---

### Q53. MHT CET 2025 (5 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The equation of a line passing through the point ( $-1,2,3$ ) and perpendicular to the lines $\frac{x}{2}=\frac{y-1}{-3}=\frac{z+2}{-2}$ and $\frac{x+3}{-1}=\frac{y+3}{2}=\frac{z-1}{3}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{x+1}{5}=\frac{y-2}{-4}=\frac{z+3}{1}$
- **(B)** $\frac{x+1}{5}=\frac{y+2}{4}=\frac{z+3}{1}$
- **(C)** $\frac{x+1}{5}=\frac{y-2}{4}=\frac{z-3}{-1}$
- **(D)** $\frac{x+1}{1}=\frac{y-2}{4}=\frac{z-3}{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{x+1}{5}=\frac{y-2}{4}=\frac{z-3}{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the given lines are $2, -3, -2$ and $-1, 2, 3$.

Since the required line is perpendicular to both given lines, its direction vector is parallel to the cross product of their direction vectors.

Let the direction vector of the required line be $\vec{v}$.

$\vec{v} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 2 & -3 & -2 \\ -1 & 2 & 3 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{v} = \hat{i}(-9 - (-4)) - \hat{j}(6 - 2) + \hat{k}(4 - 3)$

$\vec{v} = -5\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + \hat{k}$

The direction ratios of the required line are $-5, -4, 1$, which are proportional to $5, 4, -1$.

The required line passes through the point $(-1, 2, 3)$.

The equation of the line is given by:

$\dfrac{x - x_1}{a} = \dfrac{y - y_1}{b} = \dfrac{z - z_1}{c}$

$\dfrac{x - (-1)}{5} = \dfrac{y - 2}{4} = \dfrac{z - 3}{-1}$

$\dfrac{x + 1}{5} = \dfrac{y - 2}{4} = \dfrac{z - 3}{-1}$

Answer: $\frac{x+1}{5}=\frac{y-2}{4}=\frac{z-3}{-1}$

</details>

---

### Q54. MHT CET 2025 (27 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The shortest distance between the lines $\frac{x+1}{3}=\frac{y-2}{2}=\frac{z+1}{2}$ and $\frac{x-2}{1}=\frac{y-2}{2}=\frac{z+3}{3}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{2}{\sqrt{69}}$ units
- **(B)** $\frac{14}{\sqrt{69}}$ units
- **(C)** $\frac{9}{\sqrt{69}}$ units
- **(D)** $\frac{1}{\sqrt{69}}$ units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{2}{\sqrt{69}}$ units**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given lines be $L_1$ and $L_2$.

For line $L_1$, the position vector of a point on it is $\vec{a}_1 = -\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}$ and its direction vector is $\vec{b}_1 = 3\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

For line $L_2$, the position vector of a point on it is $\vec{a}_2 = 2\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}$ and its direction vector is $\vec{b}_2 = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$.

The shortest distance $d$ between two skew lines is given by:
$d = \dfrac{|(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)|}{|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2|}$

First, we find $\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1$:
$\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1 = (2 - (-1))\hat{i} + (2 - 2)\hat{j} + (-3 - (-1))\hat{k} = 3\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$

Next, we find the cross product $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$:
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 3 & 2 & 2 \\ 1 & 2 & 3 \end{vmatrix}$
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \hat{i}(6 - 4) - \hat{j}(9 - 2) + \hat{k}(6 - 2) = 2\hat{i} - 7\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$

The magnitude of $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$ is:
$|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-7)^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{4 + 49 + 16} = \sqrt{69}$

Now, we find the dot product $(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)$:
$(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2) = (3)(2) + (0)(-7) + (-2)(4) = 6 + 0 - 8 = -2$

Therefore, the shortest distance is:
$d = \dfrac{|-2|}{\sqrt{69}} = \dfrac{2}{\sqrt{69}}$ units

Answer: $\frac{2}{\sqrt{69}}$ units

</details>

---

### Q55. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The line $\frac{x-1}{2}=\frac{y+2}{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1}$ intersects the XY plane and the YZ plane at points $A$ and $B$ respectively. The equation of line through the points $A$ and $B$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $[\bar{r}-(\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+0 \hat{k})] \times\left(-\hat{i}+\frac{1}{2} \hat{j}-\frac{1}{2} \hat{k}\right)=\overline{0}$
- **(B)** $[\overline{\mathrm{r}}+(\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+0 \hat{\mathrm{k}})] \times\left(-\hat{i}+\frac{1}{2} \hat{j}+\frac{1}{2} \hat{k}\right)=\overline{0}$
- **(C)** $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=(-\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+0 \hat{k})+\lambda\left(-\hat{i}+\frac{1}{2} \hat{j}-\frac{1}{2} \hat{k}\right)$
- **(D)** $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=(\hat{i}+2 \hat{j})+\lambda\left(-\hat{i}+\frac{1}{2} \hat{j}-\frac{1}{2} \hat{k}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $[\bar{r}-(\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+0 \hat{k})] \times\left(-\hat{i}+\frac{1}{2} \hat{j}-\frac{1}{2} \hat{k}\right)=\overline{0}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the given line is $\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y+2}{-1}=\dfrac{z}{1}$.

The line intersects the XY plane where $z=0$. Substituting $z=0$ into the line equation:
$\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y+2}{-1}=\dfrac{0}{1} = 0$
$\Rightarrow x-1 = 0 \Rightarrow x = 1$
$\Rightarrow y+2 = 0 \Rightarrow y = -2$
Thus, the point of intersection with the XY plane is $A(1, -2, 0)$. The position vector of point $A$ is $\vec{a} = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$.

The line intersects the YZ plane where $x=0$. Substituting $x=0$ into the line equation:
$\dfrac{0-1}{2}=\dfrac{y+2}{-1}=\dfrac{z}{1} = -\dfrac{1}{2}$
$\Rightarrow y+2 = \dfrac{1}{2} \Rightarrow y = -\dfrac{3}{2}$
$\Rightarrow z = -\dfrac{1}{2}$
Thus, the point of intersection with the YZ plane is $B\left(0, -\dfrac{3}{2}, -\dfrac{1}{2}\right)$. The position vector of point $B$ is $\vec{b} = 0\hat{i} - \dfrac{3}{2}\hat{j} - \dfrac{1}{2}\hat{k}$.

The direction vector of the line passing through $A$ and $B$ is given by $\overrightarrow{AB} = \vec{b} - \vec{a}$:
$\overrightarrow{AB} = (0 - 1)\hat{i} + \left(-\dfrac{3}{2} - (-2)\right)\hat{j} + \left(-\dfrac{1}{2} - 0\right)\hat{k}$
$\overrightarrow{AB} = -\hat{i} + \dfrac{1}{2}\hat{j} - \dfrac{1}{2}\hat{k}$

The vector equation of a line passing through a point with position vector $\vec{a}$ and parallel to a vector $\vec{m}$ is given by $(\vec{r} - \vec{a}) \times \vec{m} = \vec{0}$.

Substituting $\vec{a} = \hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$ and $\vec{m} = \overrightarrow{AB} = -\hat{i} + \dfrac{1}{2}\hat{j} - \dfrac{1}{2}\hat{k}$, we get:
$[\vec{r} - (\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 0\hat{k})] \times \left(-\hat{i} + \dfrac{1}{2}\hat{j} - \dfrac{1}{2}\hat{k}\right) = \vec{0}$

Answer: $[\bar{r}-(\hat{i}-2 \hat{j}+0 \hat{k})] \times\left(-\hat{i}+\frac{1}{2} \hat{j}-\frac{1}{2} \hat{k}\right)=\overline{0}$

</details>

---

### Q56. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The lines $\frac{6 x-6}{18}=\frac{y+1}{3}=\frac{z-1}{5}$ and $\frac{3 x+6}{12}=\frac{y-1}{3}=\frac{z+1}{2}$ are $\ldots$

**Options:**

- **(A)** intersecting at point $(1,-1,2)$
- **(B)** intersecting at right angles
- **(C)** do not intersect
- **(D)** intersecting at point $(3,1,-1)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) do not intersect**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given equations of the lines can be written in standard form as:

Line 1: $\dfrac{6(x-1)}{18} = \dfrac{y+1}{3} = \dfrac{z-1}{5}$
$\Rightarrow \dfrac{x-1}{3} = \dfrac{y+1}{3} = \dfrac{z-1}{5} = \lambda$

Line 2: $\dfrac{3(x+2)}{12} = \dfrac{y-1}{3} = \dfrac{z+1}{2}$
$\Rightarrow \dfrac{x+2}{4} = \dfrac{y-1}{3} = \dfrac{z+1}{2} = \mu$

A general point on Line 1 is $(3\lambda + 1, 3\lambda - 1, 5\lambda + 1)$.
A general point on Line 2 is $(4\mu - 2, 3\mu + 1, 2\mu - 1)$.

If the lines intersect, these points must coincide for some values of $\lambda$ and $\mu$. Equating the respective coordinates, we get:
$3\lambda + 1 = 4\mu - 2 \Rightarrow 3\lambda - 4\mu = -3$  ... (i)
$3\lambda - 1 = 3\mu + 1 \Rightarrow 3\lambda - 3\mu = 2$   ... (ii)
$5\lambda + 1 = 2\mu - 1 \Rightarrow 5\lambda - 2\mu = -2$  ... (iii)

Subtracting equation (i) from equation (ii):
$(3\lambda - 3\mu) - (3\lambda - 4\mu) = 2 - (-3)$
$\mu = 5$

Substituting $\mu = 5$ in equation (ii):
$3\lambda - 15 = 2 \Rightarrow 3\lambda = 17 \Rightarrow \lambda = \dfrac{17}{3}$

Now, substituting $\lambda = \dfrac{17}{3}$ and $\mu = 5$ in the LHS of equation (iii):
$5\left(\dfrac{17}{3}\right) - 2(5) = \dfrac{85}{3} - 10 = \dfrac{55}{3}$

Since $\dfrac{55}{3} \neq -2$, the values of $\lambda$ and $\mu$ do not satisfy the third equation.

Therefore, the lines do not intersect.

Answer: do not intersect

</details>

---

### Q57. MHT CET 2025 (26 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A triangle ABC is formed by $\mathrm{A}(1,-1,0), \mathrm{B}(3,5,3), \mathrm{C}(-11,-5,6)$. The equation of internal angle bisector of angle $A$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{(1-x)}{2}=\frac{y-(-1)}{2}=\frac{z}{3}$
- **(B)** $\frac{x+1}{2}=\frac{y-1}{2}=\frac{z}{3}$
- **(C)** $\frac{x+2}{1}=\frac{y-2}{1}=\frac{z}{3}$
- **(D)** $\frac{x-2}{1}=\frac{y+3}{2}=\frac{z}{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{(1-x)}{2}=\frac{y-(-1)}{2}=\frac{z}{3}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The position vectors of the vertices are $A(1, -1, 0)$, $B(3, 5, 3)$, and $C(-11, -5, 6)$.

The vector $\overrightarrow{AB}$ is given by:
$\overrightarrow{AB} = (3 - 1)\hat{i} + (5 - (-1))\hat{j} + (3 - 0)\hat{k} = 2\hat{i} + 6\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$

The magnitude of $\overrightarrow{AB}$ is:
$|\overrightarrow{AB}| = \sqrt{2^2 + 6^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{4 + 36 + 9} = \sqrt{49} = 7$

The vector $\overrightarrow{AC}$ is given by:
$\overrightarrow{AC} = (-11 - 1)\hat{i} + (-5 - (-1))\hat{j} + (6 - 0)\hat{k} = -12\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}$

The magnitude of $\overrightarrow{AC}$ is:
$|\overrightarrow{AC}| = \sqrt{(-12)^2 + (-4)^2 + 6^2} = \sqrt{144 + 16 + 36} = \sqrt{196} = 14$

The unit vectors along $\overrightarrow{AB}$ and $\overrightarrow{AC}$ are:
$\hat{u}_{AB} = \dfrac{2\hat{i} + 6\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}}{7}$
$\hat{u}_{AC} = \dfrac{-12\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 6\hat{k}}{14} = \dfrac{-6\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}}{7}$

The internal angle bisector of angle $A$ is along the direction of the sum of these unit vectors:
$\hat{u}_{AB} + \hat{u}_{AC} = \dfrac{2 - 6}{7}\hat{i} + \dfrac{6 - 2}{7}\hat{j} + \dfrac{3 + 3}{7}\hat{k} = \dfrac{-4}{7}\hat{i} + \dfrac{4}{7}\hat{j} + \dfrac{6}{7}\hat{k}$

The direction ratios of the internal bisector are proportional to $-4, 4, 6$, which simplifies to $-2, 2, 3$.

The equation of the line passing through $A(1, -1, 0)$ with direction ratios $-2, 2, 3$ is:
$\dfrac{x - 1}{-2} = \dfrac{y - (-1)}{2} = \dfrac{z - 0}{3}$

Multiplying the numerator and denominator of the first term by $-1$, we get:
$\dfrac{1 - x}{2} = \dfrac{y - (-1)}{2} = \dfrac{z}{3}$

Answer: $\frac{(1-x)}{2}=\frac{y-(-1)}{2}=\frac{z}{3}$

</details>

---

### Q58. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The lines
$\bar{r}=(\hat{i}+\hat{j}-\hat{k})+\lambda(3 \hat{i}-\hat{j})$ and $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=(4 \hat{\mathrm{i}}-\hat{\mathrm{k}})+\mu(2 \hat{\mathrm{i}}+3 \hat{\mathrm{k}})$ are

**Options:**

- **(A)** intersecting but not perpendicular
- **(B)** perpendicular
- **(C)** parallel
- **(D)** skew lines

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) intersecting but not perpendicular**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given lines are:
$\vec{r} = (\hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k}) + \lambda(3\hat{i} - \hat{j})$
$\vec{r} = (4\hat{i} - \hat{k}) + \mu(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{k})$

The direction vectors of the lines are $\vec{b}_1 = 3\hat{i} - \hat{j}$ and $\vec{b}_2 = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{k}$.

Since $\vec{b}_1$ and $\vec{b}_2$ are not scalar multiples of each other, the lines are not parallel.

The dot product of the direction vectors is:
$\vec{b}_1 \cdot \vec{b}_2 = (3)(2) + (-1)(0) + (0)(3) = 6 \neq 0$
Thus, the lines are not perpendicular.

To check if they intersect, we equate their general points:
$(1 + 3\lambda)\hat{i} + (1 - \lambda)\hat{j} - \hat{k} = (4 + 2\mu)\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + (-1 + 3\mu)\hat{k}$

Equating the respective components, we get:
$1 + 3\lambda = 4 + 2\mu$
$1 - \lambda = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$
$-1 = -1 + 3\mu \Rightarrow \mu = 0$

Substituting $\lambda = 1$ and $\mu = 0$ into the first equation:
$1 + 3(1) = 4 + 2(0) \Rightarrow 4 = 4$

Since the values of $\lambda$ and $\mu$ satisfy all three equations, the lines intersect at the point $(4, 0, -1)$.

Therefore, the lines are intersecting but not perpendicular.

Answer: intersecting but not perpendicular

</details>

---

### Q59. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The Cartesian equation of the plane $\bar{r}=(2 \hat{i}-3 \hat{j})+\lambda(\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}-\hat{k})+\mu(2 \hat{i}+3 \hat{j}+\hat{k})$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $5 x-4 y+z=22$
- **(B)** $5 x-3 y+z=19$
- **(C)** $5 x-3 y-z=19$
- **(D)** $5 x-4 y-z=22$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $5 x-3 y-z=19$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given equation of the plane is of the form $\vec{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda \vec{b} + \mu \vec{c}$, where $\vec{a} = 2\hat{i} - 3\hat{j}$, $\vec{b} = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - \hat{k}$, and $\vec{c} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

The normal vector to the plane is $\vec{n} = \vec{b} \times \vec{c}$.

$\vec{n} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 1 & 2 & -1 \\ 2 & 3 & 1 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{n} = \hat{i}(2 + 3) - \hat{j}(1 + 2) + \hat{k}(3 - 4) = 5\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} - \hat{k}$

The vector equation of the plane is $\vec{r} \cdot \vec{n} = \vec{a} \cdot \vec{n}$.

$\vec{a} \cdot \vec{n} = (2\hat{i} - 3\hat{j}) \cdot (5\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} - \hat{k}) = 2(5) + (-3)(-3) = 10 + 9 = 19$

Substituting $\vec{r} = x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}$, we get the Cartesian equation:

$(x\hat{i} + y\hat{j} + z\hat{k}) \cdot (5\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} - \hat{k}) = 19$

$5x - 3y - z = 19$

Answer: $5 x-3 y-z=19$

</details>

---

### Q60. MHT CET 2025 (25 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

Let $M$ and $N$ be foots of the perpendiculars drawn from the point $\mathrm{P}(a, a, a)$ on the lines $x-\mathrm{y}=0, \mathrm{z}=1$ and $x+\mathrm{y}=0, \mathrm{z}=-1$ respectively and if $\angle \mathrm{MPN}=90^{\circ}$ then $a^2=$

**Options:**

- **(A)** 1
- **(B)** 4
- **(C)** 6
- **(D)** 9

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 1**

**Detailed Solution:**

The line $L_1$ is given by $x - y = 0, z = 1$. Its parametric coordinates are $(t, t, 1)$ and its direction vector is $\vec{d_1} = (1, 1, 0)$.

Let $M$ be $(t, t, 1)$. The vector $\overrightarrow{PM} = (t-a, t-a, 1-a)$.

Since $M$ is the foot of the perpendicular from $P$ to $L_1$, $\overrightarrow{PM}$ is perpendicular to $\vec{d_1}$:

$\overrightarrow{PM} \cdot \vec{d_1} = 0$

$(t-a)(1) + (t-a)(1) + (1-a)(0) = 0 \Rightarrow 2(t-a) = 0 \Rightarrow t = a$

Thus, $M = (a, a, 1)$ and $\overrightarrow{PM} = (0, 0, 1-a)$.

The line $L_2$ is given by $x + y = 0, z = -1$. Its parametric coordinates are $(s, -s, -1)$ and its direction vector is $\vec{d_2} = (1, -1, 0)$.

Let $N$ be $(s, -s, -1)$. The vector $\overrightarrow{PN} = (s-a, -s-a, -1-a)$.

Since $N$ is the foot of the perpendicular from $P$ to $L_2$, $\overrightarrow{PN}$ is perpendicular to $\vec{d_2}$:

$\overrightarrow{PN} \cdot \vec{d_2} = 0$

$(s-a)(1) + (-s-a)(-1) + (-1-a)(0) = 0 \Rightarrow s - a + s + a = 0 \Rightarrow 2s = 0 \Rightarrow s = 0$

Thus, $N = (0, 0, -1)$ and $\overrightarrow{PN} = (-a, -a, -1-a)$.

We are given that $\angle MPN = 90^{\circ}$, which implies $\overrightarrow{PM} \cdot \overrightarrow{PN} = 0$:

$(0)(-a) + (0)(-a) + (1-a)(-1-a) = 0$

$-(1-a)(1+a) = 0$

$a^2 - 1 = 0 \Rightarrow a^2 = 1$

Answer: 1

</details>

---

### Q61. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The line passing through the points $(a, 1,6)$ and $(3,4, b)$ crosses the yz -plane at $\left(0, \frac{17}{2}, \frac{-13}{2}\right)$, then the value of $(3 a+4 b)$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 19
- **(B)** 16
- **(C)** 21
- **(D)** 23

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 19**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the point $P\left(0, \dfrac{17}{2}, \dfrac{-13}{2}\right)$ divide the line segment joining $A(a, 1, 6)$ and $B(3, 4, b)$ in the ratio $k:1$.

Using the section formula, the coordinates of $P$ are $\left(\dfrac{3k+a}{k+1}, \dfrac{4k+1}{k+1}, \dfrac{bk+6}{k+1}\right)$.

Comparing the coordinates with $P\left(0, \dfrac{17}{2}, \dfrac{-13}{2}\right)$:

$\dfrac{3k+a}{k+1} = 0 \Rightarrow a = -3k$

$\dfrac{4k+1}{k+1} = \dfrac{17}{2} \Rightarrow 8k + 2 = 17k + 17 \Rightarrow 9k = -15 \Rightarrow k = -\dfrac{5}{3}$

Substituting $k = -\dfrac{5}{3}$ in $a = -3k$:

$a = -3\left(-\dfrac{5}{3}\right) = 5$

Comparing the z-coordinates:

$\dfrac{bk+6}{k+1} = -\dfrac{13}{2}$

Substituting $k = -\dfrac{5}{3}$:

$\dfrac{b\left(-\dfrac{5}{3}\right)+6}{-\dfrac{5}{3}+1} = -\dfrac{13}{2}$

$\dfrac{-5b+18}{-2} = -\dfrac{13}{2}$

$-5b + 18 = 13 \Rightarrow 5b = 5 \Rightarrow b = 1$

The value of $3a + 4b = 3(5) + 4(1) = 15 + 4 = 19$.

Answer: 19

</details>

---

### Q62. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The equation of the plane containing the line $\frac{x+1}{2}=\frac{y+2}{1}=\frac{z-2}{3}$ and the point $(1,-1,3)$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $x-2 y-3=0$
- **(B)** $2 x+y-1=0$
- **(C)** $3 x-2 z+3=0$
- **(D)** $2 x-y-z=0$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $x-2 y-3=0$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The plane contains the line $\dfrac{x+1}{2}=\dfrac{y+2}{1}=\dfrac{z-2}{3}$.

Thus, the plane passes through the point $A(-1, -2, 2)$ and is parallel to the vector $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$.

The plane also contains the point $B(1, -1, 3)$.

The vector joining $A$ and $B$ is $\overrightarrow{AB} = (1 - (-1))\hat{i} + (-1 - (-2))\hat{j} + (3 - 2)\hat{k} = 2\hat{i} + \hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

The normal vector $\vec{n}$ to the plane is perpendicular to both $\overrightarrow{AB}$ and $\vec{b}$.

$\vec{n} = \overrightarrow{AB} \times \vec{b} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 2 & 1 & 1 \\ 2 & 1 & 3 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{n} = \hat{i}(3 - 1) - \hat{j}(6 - 2) + \hat{k}(2 - 2) = 2\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$

The direction ratios of the normal are $(2, -4, 0)$, which can be simplified to $(1, -2, 0)$.

The equation of the plane passing through $B(1, -1, 3)$ with normal vector $\hat{i} - 2\hat{j}$ is:

$1(x - 1) - 2(y - (-1)) + 0(z - 3) = 0$

$x - 1 - 2y - 2 = 0$

$x - 2y - 3 = 0$

Answer: $x-2 y-3=0$

</details>

---

### Q63. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

The equation of the plane containing the line $\frac{x}{1}=\frac{y}{2}=\frac{z}{3}$ and perpendicular to the plane containing the lines $\frac{x}{2}=\frac{y}{3}=\frac{z}{1}$ and $\frac{x}{3}=\frac{y}{2}=\frac{z}{1}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $x-13 y+z=0$
- **(B)** $13 x-8 y+5 z=0$
- **(C)** $13 x-8 y+z=0$
- **(D)** $13 x-y+z=0$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $13 x-8 y+z=0$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the lines in the first plane are $(2, 3, 1)$ and $(3, 2, 1)$.

The normal vector $\vec{n_1}$ to the plane containing these lines is given by their cross product:

$\vec{n_1} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 2 & 3 & 1 \\ 3 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = \hat{i}(3 - 2) - \hat{j}(2 - 3) + \hat{k}(4 - 9) = \hat{i} + \hat{j} - 5\hat{k}$

The required plane contains the line $\dfrac{x}{1} = \dfrac{y}{2} = \dfrac{z}{3}$, which passes through the origin $(0, 0, 0)$ and has direction ratios $(1, 2, 3)$.

Since the required plane is perpendicular to the first plane, its normal vector $\vec{n}$ is perpendicular to both $(1, 2, 3)$ and $\vec{n_1} = (1, 1, -5)$.

$\vec{n} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 1 & 2 & 3 \\ 1 & 1 & -5 \end{vmatrix} = \hat{i}(-10 - 3) - \hat{j}(-5 - 3) + \hat{k}(1 - 2) = -13\hat{i} + 8\hat{j} - \hat{k}$

The equation of the required plane passing through the origin with normal vector $(-13, 8, -1)$ is:

$-13(x - 0) + 8(y - 0) - 1(z - 0) = 0$

$\Rightarrow 13x - 8y + z = 0$

Answer: $13 x-8 y+z=0$

</details>

---

### Q64. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\frac{x-1}{2}=\frac{y+1}{3}=\frac{z-1}{4}$ and $\frac{x-3}{1}=\frac{y-k}{2}=\frac{z}{1}$ intersect, then the value of $k$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{3}{2}$
- **(B)** $-\frac{3}{2}$
- **(C)** $\frac{9}{2}$
- **(D)** $-\frac{2}{9}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{9}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The condition for two lines $\dfrac{x-x_1}{a_1} = \dfrac{y-y_1}{b_1} = \dfrac{z-z_1}{c_1}$ and $\dfrac{x-x_2}{a_2} = \dfrac{y-y_2}{b_2} = \dfrac{z-z_2}{c_2}$ to intersect is that they must be coplanar.

$\begin{vmatrix} x_2 - x_1 & y_2 - y_1 & z_2 - z_1 \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Substituting the given values $(x_1, y_1, z_1) = (1, -1, 1)$, $(x_2, y_2, z_2) = (3, k, 0)$, $(a_1, b_1, c_1) = (2, 3, 4)$, and $(a_2, b_2, c_2) = (1, 2, 1)$:

$\begin{vmatrix} 3 - 1 & k - (-1) & 0 - 1 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

$\begin{vmatrix} 2 & k+1 & -1 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$2(3 \times 1 - 4 \times 2) - (k+1)(2 \times 1 - 4 \times 1) + (-1)(2 \times 2 - 3 \times 1) = 0$

$2(3 - 8) - (k+1)(2 - 4) - 1(4 - 3) = 0$

$2(-5) - (k+1)(-2) - 1(1) = 0$

$-10 + 2(k+1) - 1 = 0$

$2k + 2 - 11 = 0$

$2k - 9 = 0$

$k = \dfrac{9}{2}$

Answer: $\frac{9}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q65. MHT CET 2025 (23 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The equation of the line passing through the point of intersection of $\frac{x-1}{2}=\frac{y-2}{3}=\frac{z-3}{4}$ and $\frac{x-4}{5}=\frac{y-1}{2}=z$ and also through the point $(2,1,-2)$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=(-\hat{i}-\hat{\mathrm{j}}-\hat{\mathrm{k}})+\lambda(\hat{i}+2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}})$
- **(B)** $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=(-\hat{i}-\hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}})+\lambda(2 \hat{i}+2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}})$
- **(C)** $\frac{x+1}{3}=\frac{y+1}{2}=\frac{z+1}{-1}$
- **(D)** $\frac{x-1}{3}=\frac{y-1}{2}=\frac{z+1}{1}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{x+1}{3}=\frac{y+1}{2}=\frac{z+1}{-1}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the point of intersection of the given lines be $P$.

Any point on the first line $\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y-2}{3}=\dfrac{z-3}{4} = \lambda$ is $(2\lambda+1, 3\lambda+2, 4\lambda+3)$.

Any point on the second line $\dfrac{x-4}{5}=\dfrac{y-1}{2}=\dfrac{z}{1} = \mu$ is $(5\mu+4, 2\mu+1, \mu)$.

For the point of intersection, these two points must coincide:

$2\lambda+1 = 5\mu+4 \Rightarrow 2\lambda - 5\mu = 3$

$3\lambda+2 = 2\mu+1 \Rightarrow 3\lambda - 2\mu = -1$

$4\lambda+3 = \mu \Rightarrow 4\lambda - \mu = -3$

Solving these equations, we get $\lambda = -1$ and $\mu = -1$.

Substituting $\lambda = -1$ in the coordinates of the first point, the point of intersection is $P(-1, -1, -1)$.

The required line passes through $P(-1, -1, -1)$ and the given point $Q(2, 1, -2)$.

The direction ratios of the line $PQ$ are $(2 - (-1), 1 - (-1), -2 - (-1)) = (3, 2, -1)$.

The equation of the line passing through $(-1, -1, -1)$ with direction ratios $(3, 2, -1)$ is:

$\dfrac{x+1}{3}=\dfrac{y+1}{2}=\dfrac{z+1}{-1}$

Answer: $\frac{x+1}{3}=\frac{y+1}{2}=\frac{z+1}{-1}$

</details>

---

### Q66. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The angle between the lines $x-3 \mathrm{y}-4=0,4 \mathrm{y}-\mathrm{z}+5=0$ and $x+3 \mathrm{y}-11=0,2 \mathrm{y}-\mathrm{z}+6=0$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\pi}{2}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\pi}{4}$
- **(C)** $\frac{\pi}{6}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\pi}{3}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{\pi}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

For the first line, the equations are $x - 3y - 4 = 0$ and $4y - z + 5 = 0$.
Expressing $x$ and $z$ in terms of $y$, we get $x = 3y + 4$ and $z = 4y + 5$.
Let $y = t$, then $x = 3t + 4$, $y = t$, and $z = 4t + 5$.
The direction ratios of the first line are $a_1 = 3$, $b_1 = 1$, $c_1 = 4$.

For the second line, the equations are $x + 3y - 11 = 0$ and $2y - z + 6 = 0$.
Expressing $x$ and $z$ in terms of $y$, we get $x = -3y + 11$ and $z = 2y + 6$.
Let $y = s$, then $x = -3s + 11$, $y = s$, and $z = 2s + 6$.
The direction ratios of the second line are $a_2 = -3$, $b_2 = 1$, $c_2 = 2$.

The angle $\theta$ between the two lines is given by:
$\cos \theta = \dfrac{|a_1 a_2 + b_1 b_2 + c_1 c_2|}{\sqrt{a_1^2 + b_1^2 + c_1^2} \sqrt{a_2^2 + b_2^2 + c_2^2}}$

Calculating the dot product of the direction ratios:
$a_1 a_2 + b_1 b_2 + c_1 c_2 = (3)(-3) + (1)(1) + (4)(2) = -9 + 1 + 8 = 0$

Since the dot product is zero, $\cos \theta = 0$.
Therefore, $\theta = \dfrac{\pi}{2}$.

Answer: $\frac{\pi}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q67. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

If the point ($1, \alpha, \beta$) lies on the line of the shortest distance between the lines $\frac{x+2}{-3}=\frac{y-2}{4}=\frac{z-5}{2}$ and $\frac{x+2}{-1}=\frac{y+6}{2}, z=1$, then $\alpha+\beta=$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1$
- **(B)** $-3$
- **(C)** $7$
- **(D)** $-7$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given lines be $L_1$ and $L_2$.

The direction vector of $L_1$ is $\vec{b_1} = -3\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

The equation of $L_2$ can be written as $\dfrac{x+2}{-1} = \dfrac{y+6}{2} = \dfrac{z-1}{0}$.

The direction vector of $L_2$ is $\vec{b_2} = -\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 0\hat{k}$.

The line of shortest distance is perpendicular to both $L_1$ and $L_2$. Its direction vector $\vec{n}$ is given by the cross product of $\vec{b_1}$ and $\vec{b_2}$:

$\vec{n} = \vec{b_1} \times \vec{b_2} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ -3 & 4 & 2 \\ -1 & 2 & 0 \end{vmatrix} = -4\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} - 2\hat{k}$

The direction ratios of the line of shortest distance are proportional to $(2, 1, 1)$.

Let the line of shortest distance intersect $L_1$ at $P$ and $L_2$ at $Q$.

Any point on $L_1$ is $P(-3\lambda - 2, 4\lambda + 2, 2\lambda + 5)$.

Any point on $L_2$ is $Q(-\mu - 2, 2\mu - 6, 1)$.

The direction ratios of the vector $\overrightarrow{PQ}$ are $(3\lambda - \mu, -4\lambda + 2\mu - 8, -2\lambda - 4)$.

Since $\overrightarrow{PQ}$ is parallel to the line of shortest distance, its direction ratios must be proportional to $(2, 1, 1)$:

$\dfrac{3\lambda - \mu}{2} = \dfrac{-4\lambda + 2\mu - 8}{1} = \dfrac{-2\lambda - 4}{1}$

Equating the last two expressions:

$-4\lambda + 2\mu - 8 = -2\lambda - 4$

$2\mu - 2\lambda = 4 \Rightarrow \mu = \lambda + 2$

Equating the first and last expressions and substituting $\mu = \lambda + 2$:

$\dfrac{3\lambda - (\lambda + 2)}{2} = -2\lambda - 4$

$\dfrac{2\lambda - 2}{2} = -2\lambda - 4$

$\lambda - 1 = -2\lambda - 4$

$3\lambda = -3 \Rightarrow \lambda = -1$

Substitute $\lambda = -1$ to find the coordinates of $P$:

$P = (-3(-1) - 2, 4(-1) + 2, 2(-1) + 5) = (1, -2, 3)$

The equation of the line of shortest distance passing through $P(1, -2, 3)$ with direction ratios $(2, 1, 1)$ is:

$\dfrac{x - 1}{2} = \dfrac{y + 2}{1} = \dfrac{z - 3}{1}$

The point $(1, \alpha, \beta)$ lies on this line. Substituting $x = 1, y = \alpha, z = \beta$:

$\dfrac{1 - 1}{2} = \dfrac{\alpha + 2}{1} = \dfrac{\beta - 3}{1}$

$0 = \alpha + 2 = \beta - 3$

$\alpha = -2$ and $\beta = 3$

Therefore, $\alpha + \beta = -2 + 3 = 1$.

Answer: $1$

</details>

---

### Q68. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

The line $L$ is passing through $(1,2,3)$. The distance of any point on the line $L$ from the line $\bar{r}=(3 \lambda-1) \hat{i}+(-2 \lambda+3) \hat{j}+(4+\lambda) \hat{k}$ is constant. Then the line $L$ does not pass through the point

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(4,0,4)$
- **(B)** $(-2,4,2)$
- **(C)** $(7,-2,5)$
- **(D)** $(-5,6,2)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $(-5,6,2)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Since the distance of any point on the line $L$ from the given line is constant, the line $L$ must be parallel to the given line.

The equation of the given line is $\vec{r} = (-\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}) + \lambda(3\hat{i} - 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$.

The direction ratios of the given line are $(3, -2, 1)$.

Since line $L$ is parallel to the given line, its direction ratios are also $(3, -2, 1)$.

The line $L$ passes through the point $(1, 2, 3)$. The Cartesian equation of line $L$ is:

$\dfrac{x - 1}{3} = \dfrac{y - 2}{-2} = \dfrac{z - 3}{1} = t$

Any point on the line $L$ is of the form $(3t + 1, -2t + 2, t + 3)$.

Checking the given options:

For $(4, 0, 4)$, substituting $x = 4$ gives $3t + 1 = 4 \Rightarrow t = 1$. For $t = 1$, $y = 0$ and $z = 4$. This point lies on $L$.

For $(-2, 4, 2)$, substituting $x = -2$ gives $3t + 1 = -2 \Rightarrow t = -1$. For $t = -1$, $y = 4$ and $z = 2$. This point lies on $L$.

For $(7, -2, 5)$, substituting $x = 7$ gives $3t + 1 = 7 \Rightarrow t = 2$. For $t = 2$, $y = -2$ and $z = 5$. This point lies on $L$.

For $(-5, 6, 2)$, substituting $x = -5$ gives $3t + 1 = -5 \Rightarrow t = -2$. For $t = -2$, $y = 6$ but $z = -2 + 3 = 1 \neq 2$.

Thus, the point $(-5, 6, 2)$ does not lie on the line $L$.

Answer: $(-5,6,2)$

</details>

---

### Q69. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let the line $\frac{x-2}{3}=\frac{y-1}{-5}=\frac{z+2}{2}$ lie in the plane $x+3 y-\alpha z+\beta=0$, then the value of $(\beta-\alpha)$ is equal to

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1$
- **(B)** $13$
- **(C)** $7$
- **(D)** $-6$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $13$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Since the line lies in the plane, the direction vector of the line is perpendicular to the normal vector of the plane.

Direction vector of the line is $3\hat{i} - 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$ and the normal vector of the plane is $\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - \alpha\hat{k}$.

Taking their dot product to be zero:

$3(1) + (-5)(3) + 2(-\alpha) = 0$

$3 - 15 - 2\alpha = 0$

$-12 - 2\alpha = 0$

$\alpha = -6$

Also, any point on the line must lie on the plane. The point $(2, 1, -2)$ lies on the line, so it must satisfy the equation of the plane.

$2 + 3(1) - (-6)(-2) + \beta = 0$

$2 + 3 - 12 + \beta = 0$

$-7 + \beta = 0$

$\beta = 7$

Therefore, the value of $(\beta - \alpha)$ is:

$\beta - \alpha = 7 - (-6) = 13$

Answer: $13$

</details>

---

### Q70. MHT CET 2025 (22 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

The perimeter a square whose two sides have equations $\frac{x-1}{2}=\frac{y+2}{3}=\frac{z-3}{4}$ and $\frac{x}{2}=\frac{y-1}{3}=\frac{z+1}{4}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{\sqrt{673}}{\sqrt{29}}$ units
- **(B)** $\frac{4 \sqrt{673}}{\sqrt{29}}$ units
- **(C)** $\frac{4 \sqrt{573}}{\sqrt{29}}$ units
- **(D)** $\frac{4}{\sqrt{29}}$ units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{4 \sqrt{673}}{\sqrt{29}}$ units**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given equations of the lines are $\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y+2}{3}=\dfrac{z-3}{4}$ and $\dfrac{x}{2}=\dfrac{y-1}{3}=\dfrac{z+1}{4}$.

The direction ratios of both lines are proportional to $2, 3, 4$, so the lines are parallel. The distance between these parallel lines represents the side length of the square.

Let $A(1, -2, 3)$ be a point on the first line and $B(0, 1, -1)$ be a point on the second line.

The direction vector of the lines is $\vec{d} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$.

The vector joining $A$ and $B$ is $\overrightarrow{AB} = (0-1)\hat{i} + (1-(-2))\hat{j} + (-1-3)\hat{k} = -\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} - 4\hat{k}$.

The distance $a$ between the parallel lines is given by $a = \dfrac{|\overrightarrow{AB} \times \vec{d}|}{|\vec{d}|}$.

$\overrightarrow{AB} \times \vec{d} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ -1 & 3 & -4 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \end{vmatrix} = \hat{i}(12 + 12) - \hat{j}(-4 + 8) + \hat{k}(-3 - 6) = 24\hat{i} - 4\hat{j} - 9\hat{k}$.

$|\overrightarrow{AB} \times \vec{d}| = \sqrt{24^2 + (-4)^2 + (-9)^2} = \sqrt{576 + 16 + 81} = \sqrt{673}$.

$|\vec{d}| = \sqrt{2^2 + 3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{4 + 9 + 16} = \sqrt{29}$.

The side length of the square is $a = \dfrac{\sqrt{673}}{\sqrt{29}}$.

The perimeter of the square is $4a = \dfrac{4\sqrt{673}}{\sqrt{29}}$ units.

Answer: $\frac{4 \sqrt{673}}{\sqrt{29}}$ units

</details>

---

### Q71. MHT CET 2025 (21 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The line passing through the point $(5,1, a)$ and $(3, b, 1)$ crosses the yz-plane at $\left(0, \frac{17}{2}, \frac{-13}{2}\right)$, then the value of $2 a+3 \mathrm{~b}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $10$
- **(B)** $12$
- **(C)** $22$
- **(D)** $24$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $24$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the point $C\left(0, \dfrac{17}{2}, \dfrac{-13}{2}\right)$ divide the line segment joining $A(5, 1, a)$ and $B(3, b, 1)$ in the ratio $k:1$.

Using the section formula, the x-coordinate of $C$ is:

$\dfrac{3k + 5}{k + 1} = 0 \Rightarrow 3k + 5 = 0 \Rightarrow k = -\dfrac{5}{3}$

The y-coordinate of $C$ is $\dfrac{17}{2}$:

$\dfrac{bk + 1}{k + 1} = \dfrac{17}{2}$

Substituting $k = -\dfrac{5}{3}$:

$\dfrac{b\left(-\dfrac{5}{3}\right) + 1}{-\dfrac{5}{3} + 1} = \dfrac{17}{2}$

$\dfrac{-5b + 3}{-2} = \dfrac{17}{2} \Rightarrow 5b - 3 = 17 \Rightarrow 5b = 20 \Rightarrow b = 4$

The z-coordinate of $C$ is $\dfrac{-13}{2}$:

$\dfrac{1 \cdot k + a}{k + 1} = \dfrac{-13}{2}$

Substituting $k = -\dfrac{5}{3}$:

$\dfrac{-\dfrac{5}{3} + a}{-\dfrac{5}{3} + 1} = \dfrac{-13}{2}$

$\dfrac{-5 + 3a}{-2} = \dfrac{-13}{2} \Rightarrow 5 - 3a = -13 \Rightarrow 3a = 18 \Rightarrow a = 6$

The value of $2a + 3b$ is:

$2(6) + 3(4) = 12 + 12 = 24$

Answer: $24$

</details>

---

### Q72. MHT CET 2025 (21 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The equation of the plane containing the line $\frac{x-2}{3}=\frac{y+1}{2}=\frac{z-4}{-2}$ and the point $(0,5,0)$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 x-4 y-3 z+20=0$
- **(B)** $2 x+8 y+11 z-40=0$
- **(C)** $8 x-5 y+z+25=0$
- **(D)** $x-4 y+3 z+20=0$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $2 x+8 y+11 z-40=0$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given line passes through the point $A(2, -1, 4)$ and has direction ratios $3, 2, -2$.

The plane contains the line and the point $B(0, 5, 0)$.

The vector joining the points $A$ and $B$ is $\overrightarrow{AB} = (0-2)\hat{i} + (5-(-1))\hat{j} + (0-4)\hat{k} = -2\hat{i} + 6\hat{j} - 4\hat{k}$.

The normal vector $\vec{n}$ to the plane is perpendicular to both the line and the vector $\overrightarrow{AB}$.

$\vec{n} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 3 & 2 & -2 \\ -2 & 6 & -4 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{n} = \hat{i}(-8 - (-12)) - \hat{j}(-12 - 4) + \hat{k}(18 - (-4))$

$\vec{n} = 4\hat{i} + 16\hat{j} + 22\hat{k}$

Dividing by $2$, the direction ratios of the normal are $2, 8, 11$.

The equation of the plane passing through $B(0, 5, 0)$ with normal direction ratios $2, 8, 11$ is:

$2(x - 0) + 8(y - 5) + 11(z - 0) = 0$

$2x + 8y + 11z - 40 = 0$

Answer: $2 x+8 y+11 z-40=0$

</details>

---

### Q73. MHT CET 2025 (21 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The shortest distance between the lines
$\begin{aligned} & \overline{\mathrm{r}}=(4 \hat{i}-\hat{j})+\lambda(\hat{i}+2 \hat{j}-3 \hat{k}) \text { and } \\ & \overline{\mathrm{r}}=(\hat{i}-\hat{j}+2 \hat{k})+\mu(2 \hat{i}+4 \hat{j}-5 \hat{k}) \text { is }\end{aligned}$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}$ units
- **(B)** $\frac{6}{\sqrt{5}}$ units
- **(C)** $\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}$ units
- **(D)** $\frac{3}{\sqrt{5}}$ units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{6}{\sqrt{5}}$ units**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equations of the given lines are $\vec{r} = \vec{a}_1 + \lambda \vec{b}_1$ and $\vec{r} = \vec{a}_2 + \mu \vec{b}_2$, where:
$\vec{a}_1 = 4\hat{i} - \hat{j}$
$\vec{b}_1 = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} - 3\hat{k}$
$\vec{a}_2 = \hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$
$\vec{b}_2 = 2\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} - 5\hat{k}$

The shortest distance between two skew lines is given by the formula:
$d = \left| \dfrac{(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)}{|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2|} \right|$

First, find the vector $\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1$:
$\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1 = (\hat{i} - \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}) - (4\hat{i} - \hat{j}) = -3\hat{i} + 2\hat{k}$

Next, find the cross product $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$:
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 1 & 2 & -3 \\ 2 & 4 & -5 \end{vmatrix}$
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \hat{i}(-10 - (-12)) - \hat{j}(-5 - (-6)) + \hat{k}(4 - 4)$
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = 2\hat{i} - \hat{j}$

Find the magnitude of $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$:
$|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-1)^2 + 0^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1} = \sqrt{5}$

Now, find the dot product $(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)$:
$(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2) = (-3\hat{i} + 2\hat{k}) \cdot (2\hat{i} - \hat{j})$
$= (-3)(2) + (0)(-1) + (2)(0) = -6$

Finally, calculate the shortest distance:
$d = \left| \dfrac{-6}{\sqrt{5}} \right| = \dfrac{6}{\sqrt{5}}$

Answer: $\frac{6}{\sqrt{5}}$ units

</details>

---

### Q74. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the line $\frac{x+1}{1}=\frac{y-k}{11}=\frac{z-4}{-5}$ lies in the plane $2 x+p y+7 z-41=0$ which is perpendicular to the plane $x+4 \mathrm{y}-2 \mathrm{z}+13=0$ then $\mathrm{k}=$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $3$
- **(B)** $-3$
- **(C)** $-5$
- **(D)** 5

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) 5**

**Detailed Solution:**

The plane $2x + py + 7z - 41 = 0$ is perpendicular to the plane $x + 4y - 2z + 13 = 0$.

The dot product of their normal vectors must be zero:

$(2)(1) + (p)(4) + (7)(-2) = 0$

$2 + 4p - 14 = 0$

$4p = 12 \Rightarrow p = 3$

The equation of the first plane becomes $2x + 3y + 7z - 41 = 0$.

Since the line $\dfrac{x+1}{1} = \dfrac{y-k}{11} = \dfrac{z-4}{-5}$ lies in this plane, any point on the line must satisfy the equation of the plane.

A point on the line is $(-1, k, 4)$. Substituting this point into the plane's equation:

$2(-1) + 3(k) + 7(4) - 41 = 0$

$-2 + 3k + 28 - 41 = 0$

$3k - 15 = 0$

$3k = 15 \Rightarrow k = 5$

Answer: 5

</details>

---

### Q75. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The co-ordinates of the point where the line joining the points $(2,-3,1)$ and $(3,-4,-5)$ and intersects the plane $2 x+y+z=7$ are

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(-1,2,7)$
- **(B)** $(1,2,3)$
- **(C)** $(2,1,2)$
- **(D)** $(1,-2,7)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $(1,-2,7)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The equation of the line passing through the points $(2,-3,1)$ and $(3,-4,-5)$ is given by

$\dfrac{x-2}{3-2} = \dfrac{y+3}{-4-(-3)} = \dfrac{z-1}{-5-1}$

$\dfrac{x-2}{1} = \dfrac{y+3}{-1} = \dfrac{z-1}{-6} = \lambda$

Any point on this line is of the form $(\lambda+2, -\lambda-3, -6\lambda+1)$.

Since this point lies on the plane $2x+y+z=7$, we substitute the coordinates into the equation of the plane:

$2(\lambda+2) + (-\lambda-3) + (-6\lambda+1) = 7$

$2\lambda + 4 - \lambda - 3 - 6\lambda + 1 = 7$

$-5\lambda + 2 = 7$

$-5\lambda = 5 \Rightarrow \lambda = -1$

Substituting $\lambda = -1$ into the coordinates of the point, we get:

$x = -1+2 = 1$

$y = -(-1)-3 = -2$

$z = -6(-1)+1 = 7$

The required point of intersection is $(1, -2, 7)$.

Answer: $(1,-2,7)$

</details>

---

### Q76. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The distance of the point $(2,4,0)$ from the point of intersection of the lines $\frac{x+6}{3}=\frac{y}{2}=\frac{z+1}{1}$ and $\frac{x-7}{4}=\frac{y-9}{3}=\frac{z-4}{2}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 3 units
- **(B)** $3 \sqrt{3}$ units
- **(C)** 2 units
- **(D)** $2 \sqrt{3}$ units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 3 units**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the point of intersection be $Q$.

Any point on the first line is given by $\dfrac{x+6}{3} = \dfrac{y}{2} = \dfrac{z+1}{1} = \lambda$

$x = 3\lambda - 6$, $y = 2\lambda$, $z = \lambda - 1$

Any point on the second line is given by $\dfrac{x-7}{4} = \dfrac{y-9}{3} = \dfrac{z-4}{2} = \mu$

$x = 4\mu + 7$, $y = 3\mu + 9$, $z = 2\mu + 4$

For the point of intersection, the coordinates must be equal:

$3\lambda - 6 = 4\mu + 7 \Rightarrow 3\lambda - 4\mu = 13$

$2\lambda = 3\mu + 9 \Rightarrow 2\lambda - 3\mu = 9$

$\lambda - 1 = 2\mu + 4 \Rightarrow \lambda - 2\mu = 5$

Solving $2\lambda - 3\mu = 9$ and $\lambda - 2\mu = 5$, we get $\lambda = 3$ and $\mu = -1$.

Substituting $\lambda = 3$ in the coordinates of the first line, the point of intersection is $Q(3, 6, 2)$.

The given point is $P(2, 4, 0)$.

The distance between $P$ and $Q$ is:

$PQ = \sqrt{(3 - 2)^2 + (6 - 4)^2 + (2 - 0)^2}$

$PQ = \sqrt{1^2 + 2^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{1 + 4 + 4} = \sqrt{9} = 3$ units.

Answer: 3 units

</details>

---

### Q77. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If $\bar{a}=\hat{i}+\hat{j}, \overline{\mathrm{~b}}=2 \hat{i}-\hat{\mathrm{k}}$ then the point of intersection of the lines $\overline{\mathrm{r}} \times \overline{\mathrm{a}}=\overline{\mathrm{b}} \times \overline{\mathrm{a}}$ and $\overline{\mathrm{r}} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}=\overline{\mathrm{a}} \times \overline{\mathrm{b}}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(3,-1,1)$
- **(B)** $(3,1,-1)$
- **(C)** $(-3,1,1)$
- **(D)** $(1,1,1)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $(3,1,-1)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given equations of the lines are:

$\bar{r} \times \bar{a} = \bar{b} \times \bar{a}$

$\Rightarrow (\bar{r} - \bar{b}) \times \bar{a} = \bar{0}$

This implies that $(\bar{r} - \bar{b})$ is parallel to $\bar{a}$, so the equation of the first line is:

$\bar{r} = \bar{b} + \lambda \bar{a}$

Similarly, for the second line:

$\bar{r} \times \bar{b} = \bar{a} \times \bar{b}$

$\Rightarrow (\bar{r} - \bar{a}) \times \bar{b} = \bar{0}$

This implies that $(\bar{r} - \bar{a})$ is parallel to $\bar{b}$, so the equation of the second line is:

$\bar{r} = \bar{a} + \mu \bar{b}$

For the point of intersection, we equate the two position vectors:

$\bar{b} + \lambda \bar{a} = \bar{a} + \mu \bar{b}$

$\Rightarrow (\lambda - 1)\bar{a} = (\mu - 1)\bar{b}$

Since $\bar{a} = \hat{i} + \hat{j}$ and $\bar{b} = 2\hat{i} - \hat{k}$ are non-collinear vectors, the coefficients on both sides must be zero:

$\lambda - 1 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$

$\mu - 1 = 0 \Rightarrow \mu = 1$

Substituting $\lambda = 1$ into the equation of the first line, we get the position vector of the point of intersection:

$\bar{r} = \bar{a} + \bar{b}$

$\bar{r} = (\hat{i} + \hat{j}) + (2\hat{i} - \hat{k}) = 3\hat{i} + \hat{j} - \hat{k}$

Therefore, the point of intersection is $(3, 1, -1)$.

Answer: $(3,1,-1)$

</details>

---

### Q78. MHT CET 2025 (20 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the shortest distance between the lines $\frac{x-\mathrm{k}}{2}=\frac{\mathrm{y}-4}{3}=\frac{\mathrm{z}-3}{4}$ and $\frac{x-2}{4}=\frac{\mathrm{y}-4}{6}=\frac{\mathrm{z}-7}{8}$ is $\frac{13}{\sqrt{29}}$, then $\mathrm{k}=$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $1$
- **(B)** $-1$
- **(C)** $2$
- **(D)** $-2$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $1$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given lines are parallel since their direction ratios are proportional: $\dfrac{2}{4} = \dfrac{3}{6} = \dfrac{4}{8}$.

The direction vector for both lines is $\vec{b} = 2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$.

The points on the lines are $\vec{a}_1 = k\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$ and $\vec{a}_2 = 2\hat{i} + 4\hat{j} + 7\hat{k}$.

The shortest distance between two parallel lines is given by $d = \dfrac{|(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \times \vec{b}|}{|\vec{b}|}$.

We have $\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1 = (2-k)\hat{i} + 0\hat{j} + 4\hat{k}$.

Now, $(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \times \vec{b} = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 2-k & 0 & 4 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \end{vmatrix}$

$= \hat{i}(0 - 12) - \hat{j}(4(2-k) - 8) + \hat{k}(3(2-k) - 0)$

$= -12\hat{i} + 4k\hat{j} + (6-3k)\hat{k}$.

The magnitude is $|(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \times \vec{b}| = \sqrt{(-12)^2 + (4k)^2 + (6-3k)^2}$

$= \sqrt{144 + 16k^2 + 36 - 36k + 9k^2} = \sqrt{25k^2 - 36k + 180}$.

The magnitude of $\vec{b}$ is $|\vec{b}| = \sqrt{2^2 + 3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{29}$.

Given $d = \dfrac{13}{\sqrt{29}}$, we get:

$\dfrac{\sqrt{25k^2 - 36k + 180}}{\sqrt{29}} = \dfrac{13}{\sqrt{29}}$

Squaring both sides:

$25k^2 - 36k + 180 = 169$

$25k^2 - 36k + 11 = 0$

$(25k - 11)(k - 1) = 0$

$k = 1$ or $k = \dfrac{11}{25}$.

From the given options, $k = 1$.

Answer: $1$

</details>

---

### Q79. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

If the shortest distance between the lines
$\overline{\mathrm{r}}_1=\alpha \hat{i}+2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+2 \hat{\mathrm{k}}+\lambda(\hat{i}-2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+2 \hat{\mathrm{k}}), \lambda \in \mathbb{R}, \alpha>0 \text { and }$
$\overline{\mathrm{r}}_2=-4 \hat{i}-\hat{\mathrm{k}}+\mu(3 \hat{i}-2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}-2 \hat{\mathrm{k}}), \mu \in \mathrm{R}$, is 9, then the value of $\alpha$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $4$
- **(B)** $6$
- **(C)** $8$
- **(D)** $3$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $6$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given lines can be written as $\vec{r}_1 = \vec{a}_1 + \lambda \vec{b}_1$ and $\vec{r}_2 = \vec{a}_2 + \mu \vec{b}_2$, where:
$\vec{a}_1 = \alpha \hat{i} + 2 \hat{j} + 2 \hat{k}$, $\vec{b}_1 = \hat{i} - 2 \hat{j} + 2 \hat{k}$
$\vec{a}_2 = -4 \hat{i} - \hat{k}$, $\vec{b}_2 = 3 \hat{i} - 2 \hat{j} - 2 \hat{k}$

The shortest distance $d$ between the lines is given by:
$d = \dfrac{|(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)|}{|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2|}$

First, we find $\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1$:
$\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1 = (-4 - \alpha) \hat{i} - 2 \hat{j} - 3 \hat{k}$

Next, we calculate the cross product $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$:
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 1 & -2 & 2 \\ 3 & -2 & -2 \end{vmatrix}$
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \hat{i}(4 - (-4)) - \hat{j}(-2 - 6) + \hat{k}(-2 - (-6))$
$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = 8 \hat{i} + 8 \hat{j} + 4 \hat{k}$

The magnitude of $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$ is:
$|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2| = \sqrt{8^2 + 8^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{64 + 64 + 16} = \sqrt{144} = 12$

Now, we find the dot product $(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2)$:
$(\vec{a}_2 - \vec{a}_1) \cdot (\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2) = 8(-4 - \alpha) + 8(-2) + 4(-3)$
$= -32 - 8\alpha - 16 - 12$
$= -60 - 8\alpha$

Given that the shortest distance is $9$:
$\dfrac{|-60 - 8\alpha|}{12} = 9$
$|-60 - 8\alpha| = 108$
$|15 + 2\alpha| = 27$

This gives two possible equations:
$15 + 2\alpha = 27 \Rightarrow 2\alpha = 12 \Rightarrow \alpha = 6$
$15 + 2\alpha = -27 \Rightarrow 2\alpha = -42 \Rightarrow \alpha = -21$

Since it is given that $\alpha > 0$, we get $\alpha = 6$.

Answer: $6$

</details>

---

### Q80. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular drawn from a point $\mathrm{P}(-1,1,2)$ to the plane $2 x-3 y+z-11=0$

**Options:**

- **(A)** $(2,-2,1)$
- **(B)** $(2,-3,0)$
- **(C)** $(1,-2,3)$
- **(D)** $(4,1,6)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $(1,-2,3)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the foot of the perpendicular from the point $P(-1, 1, 2)$ to the given plane be $Q$.

The direction ratios of the normal to the plane $2x - 3y + z - 11 = 0$ are $2, -3, 1$.

Since the line $PQ$ is perpendicular to the plane, it is parallel to the normal vector. The equation of the line $PQ$ is:

$\dfrac{x - (-1)}{2} = \dfrac{y - 1}{-3} = \dfrac{z - 2}{1} = k$

The coordinates of any point on this line are of the form $(2k - 1, -3k + 1, k + 2)$.

Since $Q$ lies on the plane, its coordinates must satisfy the equation of the plane:

$2(2k - 1) - 3(-3k + 1) + (k + 2) - 11 = 0$

$4k - 2 + 9k - 3 + k + 2 - 11 = 0$

$14k - 14 = 0$

$k = 1$

Substituting $k = 1$ into the coordinates of $Q$, we get:

$x = 2(1) - 1 = 1$

$y = -3(1) + 1 = -2$

$z = 1 + 2 = 3$

The coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular are $(1, -2, 3)$.

Answer: $(1,-2,3)$

</details>

---

### Q81. MHT CET 2025 (19 Apr Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The distance of the point $(-3,2,3)$ from the line passing through $(4,6,-2)$ and having direction ratios $-1,2,3$ is ________ units.

**Options:**

- **(A)** $2 \sqrt{17}$
- **(B)** $4 \sqrt{17}$
- **(C)** $2 \sqrt{19}$
- **(D)** $4 \sqrt{19}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $2 \sqrt{19}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given point be $P(-3, 2, 3)$.

The equation of the line passing through $A(4, 6, -2)$ with direction ratios $-1, 2, 3$ is
$\dfrac{x-4}{-1} = \dfrac{y-6}{2} = \dfrac{z+2}{3} = \lambda$

Any point on this line can be taken as $Q(4-\lambda, 6+2\lambda, -2+3\lambda)$.

The direction ratios of the line segment $PQ$ are:
$(4-\lambda - (-3), 6+2\lambda - 2, -2+3\lambda - 3) = (7-\lambda, 4+2\lambda, -5+3\lambda)$

Since $PQ$ is perpendicular to the given line, the sum of the products of their corresponding direction ratios is zero:
$-1(7-\lambda) + 2(4+2\lambda) + 3(-5+3\lambda) = 0$

$-7 + \lambda + 8 + 4\lambda - 15 + 9\lambda = 0$

$14\lambda - 14 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = 1$

Substituting $\lambda = 1$, the coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular $Q$ are $(3, 8, 1)$.

The required distance is the length of $PQ$:
$PQ = \sqrt{(3 - (-3))^2 + (8 - 2)^2 + (1 - 3)^2}$

$PQ = \sqrt{6^2 + 6^2 + (-2)^2}$

$PQ = \sqrt{36 + 36 + 4} = \sqrt{76} = 2\sqrt{19}$ units.

Answer: $2 \sqrt{19}$

</details>

---

### Q82. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A line having direction ratios $1,-4,2$ intersects the lines $\frac{x-7}{3}=\frac{y-1}{-1}=\frac{z+2}{1}$ and $\frac{x}{2}=\frac{y-7}{3}=\frac{z}{1}$ at the points $A$ and $B$ resp., then co-ordinates of points A and B are

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{A}(-8,6,-7) \\ & \mathrm{B}(-6,-2,-3)\end{aligned}$
- **(B)** $\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{A}(8,6,7) \\ & \mathrm{B}(6,2,3)\end{aligned}$
- **(C)** $\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{A}(8,6,7) \\ & \mathrm{B}(6,-2,-3)\end{aligned}$
- **(D)** $\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{A}(7 ; 6,8) \\ & \mathrm{B}(-3,-2,6)\end{aligned}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{A}(-8,6,-7) \\ & \mathrm{B}(-6,-2,-3)\end{aligned}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let point $A$ on the first line be $(3\lambda + 7, -\lambda + 1, \lambda - 2)$.

Let point $B$ on the second line be $(2\mu, 3\mu + 7, \mu)$.

The direction ratios of the line $AB$ are $(3\lambda - 2\mu + 7, -\lambda - 3\mu - 6, \lambda - \mu - 2)$.

Since the direction ratios of the line $AB$ are proportional to $1, -4, 2$, we have:

$\dfrac{3\lambda - 2\mu + 7}{1} = \dfrac{-\lambda - 3\mu - 6}{-4} = \dfrac{\lambda - \mu - 2}{2}$

Taking the first and third terms:

$2(3\lambda - 2\mu + 7) = \lambda - \mu - 2$

$6\lambda - 4\mu + 14 = \lambda - \mu - 2$

$5\lambda - 3\mu = -16$

Taking the second and third terms:

$2(-\lambda - 3\mu - 6) = -4(\lambda - \mu - 2)$

$-\lambda - 3\mu - 6 = -2\lambda + 2\mu + 4$

$\lambda - 5\mu = 10$

Solving the two linear equations:

Substitute $\lambda = 5\mu + 10$ into $5\lambda - 3\mu = -16$:

$5(5\mu + 10) - 3\mu = -16$

$25\mu + 50 - 3\mu = -16$

$22\mu = -66 \Rightarrow \mu = -3$

Substitute $\mu = -3$ into $\lambda = 5\mu + 10$:

$\lambda = 5(-3) + 10 = -5$

Substituting $\lambda = -5$ into the coordinates of $A$:

$A \equiv (3(-5) + 7, -(-5) + 1, -5 - 2) \equiv (-8, 6, -7)$

Substituting $\mu = -3$ into the coordinates of $B$:

$B \equiv (2(-3), 3(-3) + 7, -3) \equiv (-6, -2, -3)$

Answer: $\begin{aligned} & \mathrm{A}(-8,6,-7) \\ & \mathrm{B}(-6,-2,-3)\end{aligned}$

</details>

---

### Q83. MHT CET 2024 (16 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The co-ordinates of the foot of the perpendicular from the point $(0,2,3)$ on the line $\frac{x+3}{5}=\frac{y+1}{2}=\frac{z+4}{3}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\left(\frac{48}{19}, \frac{23}{19}, \frac{-13}{19}\right)$
- **(B)** $\left(\frac{-48}{19}, \frac{23}{19}, \frac{-13}{19}\right)$
- **(C)** $\left(\frac{-48}{19}, \frac{-23}{19}, \frac{-13}{19}\right)$
- **(D)** $\left(\frac{48}{19}, \frac{-23}{19}, \frac{-13}{19}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\left(\frac{48}{19}, \frac{23}{19}, \frac{-13}{19}\right)$**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given point be $P(0,2,3)$.

Let the given line be $\dfrac{x+3}{5}=\dfrac{y+1}{2}=\dfrac{z+4}{3} = \lambda$.

Any point on the line is $Q(5\lambda - 3, 2\lambda - 1, 3\lambda - 4)$.

The direction ratios of $PQ$ are $(5\lambda - 3, 2\lambda - 3, 3\lambda - 7)$.

Since $PQ$ is perpendicular to the given line, the sum of the products of their direction ratios is zero:

$5(5\lambda - 3) + 2(2\lambda - 3) + 3(3\lambda - 7) = 0$

$25\lambda - 15 + 4\lambda - 6 + 9\lambda - 21 = 0$

$38\lambda - 42 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = \dfrac{21}{19}$

Substituting $\lambda = \dfrac{21}{19}$ in the coordinates of $Q$, we get:

$x = 5\left(\dfrac{21}{19}\right) - 3 = \dfrac{105 - 57}{19} = \dfrac{48}{19}$

$y = 2\left(\dfrac{21}{19}\right) - 1 = \dfrac{42 - 19}{19} = \dfrac{23}{19}$

$z = 3\left(\dfrac{21}{19}\right) - 4 = \dfrac{63 - 76}{19} = \dfrac{-13}{19}$

The coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular are $\left(\dfrac{48}{19}, \dfrac{23}{19}, \dfrac{-13}{19}\right)$.

Answer: $\left(\frac{48}{19}, \frac{23}{19}, \frac{-13}{19}\right)$

</details>

---

### Q84. MHT CET 2024 (15 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the lines $\frac{x-1}{2}=\frac{y+2}{3}=\frac{z-1}{4}$ and $\frac{x-3}{1}=\frac{y-\mathrm{k}}{2}=\frac{\mathrm{z}}{1}$ intersect, then k has the value

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{7}{2}$
- **(B)** $\frac{3}{2}$
- **(C)** $\frac{-7}{2}$
- **(D)** $\frac{-3}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\frac{7}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The condition for two lines $\dfrac{x-x_1}{a_1} = \dfrac{y-y_1}{b_1} = \dfrac{z-z_1}{c_1}$ and $\dfrac{x-x_2}{a_2} = \dfrac{y-y_2}{b_2} = \dfrac{z-z_2}{c_2}$ to intersect is that they must be coplanar, which means:

$\begin{vmatrix} x_2-x_1 & y_2-y_1 & z_2-z_1 \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

From the given equations of the lines, we have:
$(x_1, y_1, z_1) = (1, -2, 1)$
$(x_2, y_2, z_2) = (3, \mathrm{k}, 0)$
$(a_1, b_1, c_1) = (2, 3, 4)$
$(a_2, b_2, c_2) = (1, 2, 1)$

Substituting these values into the determinant condition:

$\begin{vmatrix} 3-1 & \mathrm{k}-(-2) & 0-1 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

$\begin{vmatrix} 2 & \mathrm{k}+2 & -1 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \\ 1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$2(3 \times 1 - 4 \times 2) - (\mathrm{k}+2)(2 \times 1 - 4 \times 1) - 1(2 \times 2 - 3 \times 1) = 0$

$2(3 - 8) - (\mathrm{k}+2)(2 - 4) - 1(4 - 3) = 0$

$2(-5) - (\mathrm{k}+2)(-2) - 1(1) = 0$

$-10 + 2(\mathrm{k}+2) - 1 = 0$

$2(\mathrm{k}+2) = 11$

$\mathrm{k}+2 = \dfrac{11}{2}$

$\mathrm{k} = \dfrac{11}{2} - 2 = \dfrac{7}{2}$

Answer: $\frac{7}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q85. MHT CET 2024 (15 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

Let $\mathrm{L}_1: \frac{x+2}{5}=\frac{y-3}{2}=\frac{z-6}{1}$ and $\mathrm{L}_2: \frac{x-3}{4}=\frac{y+2}{3}=\frac{z-3}{5}$ be the given lines, Then the unit vector perpendicular to both $\mathrm{L}_1$ and $\mathrm{L}_2$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{-\hat{i}-3 \hat{j}+\hat{k}}{\sqrt{11}}$
- **(B)** $\frac{\hat{\mathbf{i}}-3 \hat{\mathbf{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}}}{\sqrt{11}}$
- **(C)** $\frac{\hat{i}+3 \hat{j}-\hat{k}}{\sqrt{11}}$
- **(D)** $\frac{\hat{\mathrm{i}}+3 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}}}{\sqrt{11}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\frac{\hat{\mathbf{i}}-3 \hat{\mathbf{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}}}{\sqrt{11}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the line $L_1$ are $5, 2, 1$. Let its direction vector be $\vec{b}_1 = 5\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

The direction ratios of the line $L_2$ are $4, 3, 5$. Let its direction vector be $\vec{b}_2 = 4\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}$.

A vector perpendicular to both lines is given by the cross product $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$:

$\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 5 & 2 & 1 \\ 4 & 3 & 5 \end{vmatrix}$

$= \hat{i}(10 - 3) - \hat{j}(25 - 4) + \hat{k}(15 - 8)$

$= 7\hat{i} - 21\hat{j} + 7\hat{k}$

$= 7(\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

The magnitude of this vector is:

$|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2| = 7\sqrt{1^2 + (-3)^2 + 1^2} = 7\sqrt{1 + 9 + 1} = 7\sqrt{11}$

The unit vector perpendicular to both lines is:

$\hat{n} = \pm \dfrac{\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2}{|\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2|} = \pm \dfrac{7(\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + \hat{k})}{7\sqrt{11}} = \pm \dfrac{\hat{i} - 3\hat{j} + \hat{k}}{\sqrt{11}}$

Answer: $\frac{\hat{\mathbf{i}}-3 \hat{\mathbf{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}}}{\sqrt{11}}$

</details>

---

### Q86. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

A straight line L through the point $(3,-2)$ is inclined at an angle of $60^{\circ}$ to the line $\sqrt{3} x+y=1$. If L also intersects the X -axis, then the equation of $L$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $y+\sqrt{3} x+2-3 \sqrt{3}=0$
- **(B)** $y-\sqrt{3} x+2+3 \sqrt{3}=0$
- **(C)** $\sqrt{3} y-x+3+2 \sqrt{3}=0$
- **(D)** $\sqrt{3} y+x-3+2 \sqrt{3}=0$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $y-\sqrt{3} x+2+3 \sqrt{3}=0$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given line is $\sqrt{3}x + y = 1$, which has a slope $m_1 = -\sqrt{3}$.

Let the slope of the required line $L$ be $m$. The angle between the lines is $60^{\circ}$.

Using the formula for the angle between two lines:

$\tan 60^{\circ} = \left| \dfrac{m - m_1}{1 + m m_1} \right|$

$\sqrt{3} = \left| \dfrac{m - (-\sqrt{3})}{1 + m(-\sqrt{3})} \right| = \left| \dfrac{m + \sqrt{3}}{1 - \sqrt{3}m} \right|$

This gives two cases:

Case 1: $\dfrac{m + \sqrt{3}}{1 - \sqrt{3}m} = \sqrt{3}$

$m + \sqrt{3} = \sqrt{3} - 3m \Rightarrow 4m = 0 \Rightarrow m = 0$

If $m = 0$, the line is horizontal and its equation passing through $(3, -2)$ is $y = -2$. This line is parallel to the X-axis and does not intersect it, which contradicts the given condition. Thus, $m = 0$ is rejected.

Case 2: $\dfrac{m + \sqrt{3}}{1 - \sqrt{3}m} = -\sqrt{3}$

$m + \sqrt{3} = -\sqrt{3} + 3m \Rightarrow 2m = 2\sqrt{3} \Rightarrow m = \sqrt{3}$

The equation of the line $L$ passing through $(3, -2)$ with slope $m = \sqrt{3}$ is:

$y - (-2) = \sqrt{3}(x - 3)$

$y + 2 = \sqrt{3}x - 3\sqrt{3}$

$y - \sqrt{3}x + 2 + 3\sqrt{3} = 0$

Answer: $y-\sqrt{3} x+2+3 \sqrt{3}=0$

</details>

---

### Q87. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The equation of the line passing through the point $(3,1,2)$ and perpendicular to the lines $\frac{x-1}{1}=\frac{y-2}{2}=\frac{z-3}{3}$ and $\frac{x}{-3}=\frac{y}{2}=\frac{z}{5}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\frac{x+3}{2}=\frac{y+1}{7}=\frac{z+2}{4}$
- **(B)** $\frac{x-3}{-2}=\frac{y-1}{7}=\frac{z-2}{4}$
- **(C)** $\frac{x-3}{2}=\frac{y-1}{-7}=\frac{z-2}{4}$
- **(D)** $\frac{x-3}{2}=\frac{y-1}{5}=\frac{z-2}{4}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) $\frac{x-3}{2}=\frac{y-1}{-7}=\frac{z-2}{4}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction ratios of the given lines are $\vec{b}_1 = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$ and $\vec{b}_2 = -3\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}$.

The required line is perpendicular to both the given lines, so its direction vector $\vec{b}$ is parallel to $\vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2$.

$\vec{b} = \vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 1 & 2 & 3 \\ -3 & 2 & 5 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{b} = \hat{i}(10 - 6) - \hat{j}(5 - (-9)) + \hat{k}(2 - (-6))$

$\vec{b} = 4\hat{i} - 14\hat{j} + 8\hat{k}$

The direction ratios of the required line are proportional to $(4, -14, 8)$, which can be simplified to $(2, -7, 4)$.

The line passes through the point $(3, 1, 2)$.

The equation of the required line is $\dfrac{x-3}{2} = \dfrac{y-1}{-7} = \dfrac{z-2}{4}$.

Answer: $\frac{x-3}{2}=\frac{y-1}{-7}=\frac{z-2}{4}$

</details>

---

### Q88. MHT CET 2024 (11 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If the lines $\frac{x+1}{-10}=\frac{y+\mathrm{k}}{-1}=\frac{z-4}{1}$ and $\frac{x+10}{-1}=\frac{y+1}{-3}=\frac{z-1}{4}$ intersect each other, then the value of $k$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** -3
- **(B)** 3
- **(C)** 4
- **(D)** 2

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) 3**

**Detailed Solution:**

The condition for two lines $\dfrac{x-x_1}{a_1} = \dfrac{y-y_1}{b_1} = \dfrac{z-z_1}{c_1}$ and $\dfrac{x-x_2}{a_2} = \dfrac{y-y_2}{b_2} = \dfrac{z-z_2}{c_2}$ to intersect is that they must be coplanar, which means:

$\begin{vmatrix} x_2 - x_1 & y_2 - y_1 & z_2 - z_1 \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

From the given equations, we have:
$(x_1, y_1, z_1) = (-1, -\mathrm{k}, 4)$
$(x_2, y_2, z_2) = (-10, -1, 1)$
$(a_1, b_1, c_1) = (-10, -1, 1)$
$(a_2, b_2, c_2) = (-1, -3, 4)$

Substituting these values into the determinant:

$\begin{vmatrix} -10 - (-1) & -1 - (-\mathrm{k}) & 1 - 4 \\ -10 & -1 & 1 \\ -1 & -3 & 4 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

$\begin{vmatrix} -9 & \mathrm{k}-1 & -3 \\ -10 & -1 & 1 \\ -1 & -3 & 4 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$-9(-4 + 3) - (\mathrm{k}-1)(-40 + 1) - 3(30 - 1) = 0$

$-9(-1) - (\mathrm{k}-1)(-39) - 3(29) = 0$

$9 + 39(\mathrm{k}-1) - 87 = 0$

$39(\mathrm{k}-1) - 78 = 0$

$39(\mathrm{k}-1) = 78$

$\mathrm{k}-1 = 2$

$\mathrm{k} = 3$

Answer: 3

</details>

---

### Q89. MHT CET 2024 (10 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

If the length of the perpendicular to a line from the origin is $2 \sqrt{2}$ units, which makes an angle of $135^{\circ}$ with the X -axis, then the equation of line is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $x+y=4$
- **(B)** $x-y+4=0$
- **(C)** $x-y=4$
- **(D)** $x+y+4=0$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $x-y+4=0$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The normal form of the equation of a line is given by $x \cos \alpha + y \sin \alpha = p$, where $p$ is the perpendicular distance from the origin to the line and $\alpha$ is the angle made by the perpendicular with the positive X-axis.

Given $p = 2\sqrt{2}$ and $\alpha = 135^{\circ}$.

Substituting these values into the normal form:

$x \cos 135^{\circ} + y \sin 135^{\circ} = 2\sqrt{2}$

$x \left(-\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right) + y \left(\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right) = 2\sqrt{2}$

Multiplying the entire equation by $\sqrt{2}$:

$-x + y = 4$

$x - y + 4 = 0$

Answer: $x-y+4=0$

</details>

---

### Q90. MHT CET 2024 (10 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If the line $\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y+1}{3}=\dfrac{z-1}{4}$ and $\dfrac{x-3}{1}=\dfrac{y-\mathrm{k}}{2}=\dfrac{z}{1}$ intersect; then the value of k is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{3}{2}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{9}{2}$
- **(C)** $-\dfrac{2}{9}$
- **(D)** $-\dfrac{3}{2}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\dfrac{9}{2}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given lines are:

$\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y+1}{3}=\dfrac{z-1}{4}$

$\dfrac{x-3}{1}=\dfrac{y-\mathrm{k}}{2}=\dfrac{z}{1}$

Any point on the first line can be written as $(2\lambda + 1, 3\lambda - 1, 4\lambda + 1)$.

Any point on the second line can be written as $(\mu + 3, 2\mu + \mathrm{k}, \mu)$.

Since the lines intersect, these points must coincide for some values of $\lambda$ and $\mu$. Equating the $x$ and $z$ coordinates:

$2\lambda + 1 = \mu + 3 \Rightarrow 2\lambda - \mu = 2$

$4\lambda + 1 = \mu \Rightarrow 4\lambda - \mu = -1$

Subtracting the first equation from the second gives:

$2\lambda = -3 \Rightarrow \lambda = -\dfrac{3}{2}$

Substituting $\lambda = -\dfrac{3}{2}$ into the first equation:

$2\left(-\dfrac{3}{2}\right) - \mu = 2 \Rightarrow -3 - \mu = 2 \Rightarrow \mu = -5$

Now, equating the $y$ coordinates and substituting the values of $\lambda$ and $\mu$:

$3\lambda - 1 = 2\mu + \mathrm{k}$

$3\left(-\dfrac{3}{2}\right) - 1 = 2(-5) + \mathrm{k}$

$-\dfrac{9}{2} - 1 = -10 + \mathrm{k}$

$-\dfrac{11}{2} = -10 + \mathrm{k}$

$\mathrm{k} = 10 - \dfrac{11}{2} = \dfrac{9}{2}$

Answer: $\dfrac{9}{2}$

</details>

---

### Q91. MHT CET 2024 (10 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

If the lines $\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y-2}{3}=\dfrac{z-1}{4}$ and $\dfrac{x-3}{-1}=\dfrac{y-\mathrm{k}}{2}=\dfrac{\mathrm{z}}{1}$ intersect, then k is equal to

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{-5}{6}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{5}{6}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{6}{5}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{-6}{5}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\dfrac{-5}{6}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The condition for two lines $\dfrac{x-x_1}{a_1} = \dfrac{y-y_1}{b_1} = \dfrac{z-z_1}{c_1}$ and $\dfrac{x-x_2}{a_2} = \dfrac{y-y_2}{b_2} = \dfrac{z-z_2}{c_2}$ to intersect is that they must be coplanar, which means:

$\begin{vmatrix} x_2 - x_1 & y_2 - y_1 & z_2 - z_1 \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

From the given equations of the lines, we have:
$(x_1, y_1, z_1) = (1, 2, 1)$
$(x_2, y_2, z_2) = (3, \mathrm{k}, 0)$
$(a_1, b_1, c_1) = (2, 3, 4)$
$(a_2, b_2, c_2) = (-1, 2, 1)$

Substituting these values into the determinant:

$\begin{vmatrix} 3 - 1 & \mathrm{k} - 2 & 0 - 1 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \\ -1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

$\begin{vmatrix} 2 & \mathrm{k} - 2 & -1 \\ 2 & 3 & 4 \\ -1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = 0$

Expanding the determinant along the first row:

$2(3 \times 1 - 4 \times 2) - (\mathrm{k} - 2)(2 \times 1 - 4 \times (-1)) - 1(2 \times 2 - 3 \times (-1)) = 0$

$2(3 - 8) - (\mathrm{k} - 2)(2 + 4) - 1(4 + 3) = 0$

$2(-5) - 6(\mathrm{k} - 2) - 1(7) = 0$

$-10 - 6\mathrm{k} + 12 - 7 = 0$

$-6\mathrm{k} - 5 = 0$

$\mathrm{k} = \dfrac{-5}{6}$

Answer: $\dfrac{-5}{6}$

</details>

---

### Q92. MHT CET 2024 (09 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `3`

The length of the perpendicular from the point $\mathrm{A}(1,-2,-3)$ on the line $\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y+3}{-1}=\dfrac{z+1}{-2}$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** 6 units
- **(B)** 3 units
- **(C)** 2 units
- **(D)** 4 units

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(C) 2 units**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given point be $A(1, -2, -3)$.

The equation of the line is $\dfrac{x-1}{2}=\dfrac{y+3}{-1}=\dfrac{z+1}{-2} = \lambda$.

Any point on this line is $P(2\lambda + 1, -\lambda - 3, -2\lambda - 1)$.

The direction ratios of the line segment $AP$ are $(2\lambda + 1 - 1, -\lambda - 3 - (-2), -2\lambda - 1 - (-3))$, which simplifies to $(2\lambda, -\lambda - 1, -2\lambda + 2)$.

Since $AP$ is perpendicular to the given line, the dot product of their direction ratios is zero:

$2(2\lambda) - 1(-\lambda - 1) - 2(-2\lambda + 2) = 0$

$4\lambda + \lambda + 1 + 4\lambda - 4 = 0$

$9\lambda - 3 = 0 \Rightarrow \lambda = \dfrac{1}{3}$

Substituting $\lambda = \dfrac{1}{3}$, the coordinates of the foot of the perpendicular $P$ are $\left(\dfrac{5}{3}, \dfrac{-10}{3}, \dfrac{-5}{3}\right)$.

The length of the perpendicular $AP$ is:

$AP = \sqrt{\left(\dfrac{5}{3} - 1\right)^2 + \left(\dfrac{-10}{3} - (-2)\right)^2 + \left(\dfrac{-5}{3} - (-3)\right)^2}$

$AP = \sqrt{\left(\dfrac{2}{3}\right)^2 + \left(\dfrac{-4}{3}\right)^2 + \left(\dfrac{4}{3}\right)^2}$

$AP = \sqrt{\dfrac{4}{9} + \dfrac{16}{9} + \dfrac{16}{9}} = \sqrt{\dfrac{36}{9}} = \sqrt{4} = 2$ units.

Answer: 2 units

</details>

---

### Q93. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 2)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

The Cartesian equation of a line is $2 x-2=3 y+1=6 z-2$, then the vector equation of the line is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\bar{r}=\left(\hat{i}-\dfrac{\hat{j}}{3}+\dfrac{\hat{k}}{3}\right)+\lambda(3 \hat{i}+2 \hat{j}+\hat{k})$
- **(B)** $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=\left(-\hat{\mathrm{i}}+\dfrac{\hat{\mathrm{j}}}{3}-\dfrac{\hat{\mathrm{k}}}{3}\right)+\lambda\left(\dfrac{1}{2} \hat{\mathrm{i}}+\dfrac{1}{3} \hat{\mathrm{j}}+\dfrac{1}{6} \hat{\mathrm{k}}\right)$
- **(C)** $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=(3 \hat{\mathrm{i}}-\hat{\mathrm{j}}-\hat{\mathrm{k}})+\lambda(3 \hat{\mathrm{i}}+2 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}})$
- **(D)** $\overline{\mathrm{r}}=(\hat{\mathrm{i}}-\hat{\mathrm{j}}+\hat{\mathrm{k}})+\lambda\left(\dfrac{1}{2} \hat{\mathrm{i}}+\dfrac{1}{3} \hat{\mathrm{j}}+\dfrac{1}{6} \hat{\mathrm{k}}\right)$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) $\bar{r}=\left(\hat{i}-\dfrac{\hat{j}}{3}+\dfrac{\hat{k}}{3}\right)+\lambda(3 \hat{i}+2 \hat{j}+\hat{k})$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The given Cartesian equation of the line is $2x - 2 = 3y + 1 = 6z - 2$.

Rewriting the equation by factoring out the coefficients of $x, y, z$:

$2(x - 1) = 3\left(y + \dfrac{1}{3}\right) = 6\left(z - \dfrac{1}{3}\right)$

Dividing the entire equation by the LCM of $2, 3, 6$, which is $6$:

$\dfrac{x - 1}{3} = \dfrac{y + \dfrac{1}{3}}{2} = \dfrac{z - \dfrac{1}{3}}{1}$

This is the standard form of the line $\dfrac{x - x_1}{a} = \dfrac{y - y_1}{b} = \dfrac{z - z_1}{c}$.

The line passes through the point with position vector $\vec{a} = \hat{i} - \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{j} + \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}$ and is parallel to the vector $\vec{b} = 3\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k}$.

The vector equation of the line is given by $\vec{r} = \vec{a} + \lambda \vec{b}$.

$\vec{r} = \left(\hat{i} - \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{j} + \dfrac{1}{3}\hat{k}\right) + \lambda(3\hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

Answer: $\bar{r}=\left(\hat{i}-\dfrac{\hat{j}}{3}+\dfrac{\hat{k}}{3}\right)+\lambda(3 \hat{i}+2 \hat{j}+\hat{k})$

</details>

---

### Q94. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Let $a, b \in R$. If the mirror image of the point $\mathrm{p}(\mathrm{a}, 6,9)$ w.r.t. line $\dfrac{x-3}{7}=\dfrac{y-2}{5}=\dfrac{z-1}{-9}$ is $(20, b,-a-9)$, then $|a+b|$ is equal to

**Options:**

- **(A)** 88
- **(B)** 86
- **(C)** 90
- **(D)** 84

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(A) 88**

**Detailed Solution:**

Let the given point be $P(a, 6, 9)$ and its mirror image be $Q(20, b, -a-9)$.

The midpoint $M$ of $PQ$ lies on the given line $\dfrac{x-3}{7}=\dfrac{y-2}{5}=\dfrac{z-1}{-9}$.

The coordinates of $M$ are $\left(\dfrac{a+20}{2}, \dfrac{b+6}{2}, \dfrac{-a}{2}\right)$.

Substituting the coordinates of $M$ into the equation of the line:

$\dfrac{\dfrac{a+20}{2}-3}{7} = \dfrac{\dfrac{b+6}{2}-2}{5} = \dfrac{\dfrac{-a}{2}-1}{-9}$

$\dfrac{a+14}{14} = \dfrac{b+2}{10} = \dfrac{a+2}{18}$

Equating the first and third parts:

$\dfrac{a+14}{14} = \dfrac{a+2}{18}$

$9(a+14) = 7(a+2)$

$9a + 126 = 7a + 14 \Rightarrow 2a = -112 \Rightarrow a = -56$

Equating the first and second parts:

$\dfrac{-56+14}{14} = \dfrac{b+2}{10}$

$\dfrac{-42}{14} = \dfrac{b+2}{10}$

$-3 = \dfrac{b+2}{10} \Rightarrow b+2 = -30 \Rightarrow b = -32$

Therefore, $|a+b| = |-56 - 32| = |-88| = 88$.

Answer: 88

</details>

---

### Q95. MHT CET 2024 (04 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `1`

Let $\mathrm{L}_1: \dfrac{x+1}{3}=\dfrac{y+2}{1}=\dfrac{\mathrm{z}+1}{2}$ and
$\mathrm{L}_2: \dfrac{x-2}{1}=\dfrac{y+2}{2}=\dfrac{z-3}{3}$
be two given lines. Then the unit vector perpendicular to $L_1$ and $L_2$ is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{-\hat{\mathrm{i}}+7 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+7 \hat{\mathrm{k}}}{\sqrt{99}}$
- **(B)** $\dfrac{-\hat{\mathrm{i}}-7 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+5 \hat{\mathrm{k}}}{5 \sqrt{3}}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{-\hat{\mathrm{i}}+7 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+5 \hat{\mathrm{k}}}{5 \sqrt{3}}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{7 \hat{\mathrm{i}}-7 \hat{\mathrm{j}}-7 \hat{\mathrm{k}}}{\sqrt{99}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(B) $\dfrac{-\hat{\mathrm{i}}-7 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+5 \hat{\mathrm{k}}}{5 \sqrt{3}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The direction vector of line $L_1$ is $\vec{b}_1 = 3\hat{i} + \hat{j} + 2\hat{k}$.

The direction vector of line $L_2$ is $\vec{b}_2 = \hat{i} + 2\hat{j} + 3\hat{k}$.

A vector perpendicular to both $L_1$ and $L_2$ is given by the cross product of their direction vectors:

$\vec{n} = \vec{b}_1 \times \vec{b}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \hat{i} & \hat{j} & \hat{k} \\ 3 & 1 & 2 \\ 1 & 2 & 3 \end{vmatrix}$

$\vec{n} = \hat{i}(3 - 4) - \hat{j}(9 - 2) + \hat{k}(6 - 1) = -\hat{i} - 7\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}$

The magnitude of this vector is:

$|\vec{n}| = \sqrt{(-1)^2 + (-7)^2 + 5^2} = \sqrt{1 + 49 + 25} = \sqrt{75} = 5\sqrt{3}$

The unit vector perpendicular to both lines is:

$\hat{n} = \dfrac{\vec{n}}{|\vec{n}|} = \dfrac{-\hat{i} - 7\hat{j} + 5\hat{k}}{5\sqrt{3}}$

Answer: $\dfrac{-\hat{\mathrm{i}}-7 \hat{\mathrm{j}}+5 \hat{\mathrm{k}}}{5 \sqrt{3}}$

</details>

---

### Q96. MHT CET 2024 (03 May Shift 1)

*Type:* `singleCorrect` | *Level:* `2`

The line L given by $\dfrac{x}{5}+\dfrac{y}{b}=1$ passes through the point $(13,32)$. The line K is parallel to L and has the equation $\dfrac{x}{c}+\dfrac{y}{3}=1$. Then the distance between L and K is

**Options:**

- **(A)** $\dfrac{23}{\sqrt{15}}$
- **(B)** $\sqrt{17}$
- **(C)** $\dfrac{17}{\sqrt{15}}$
- **(D)** $\dfrac{23}{\sqrt{17}}$

<details>
<summary><b>View Answer & Solution</b></summary>

**Correct Option:** **(D) $\dfrac{23}{\sqrt{17}}$**

**Detailed Solution:**

The line L is given by $\dfrac{x}{5} + \dfrac{y}{b} = 1$.

Since it passes through $(13, 32)$, we substitute these coordinates into the equation:

$\dfrac{13}{5} + \dfrac{32}{b} = 1$

$\dfrac{32}{b} = 1 - \dfrac{13}{5} = -\dfrac{8}{5}$

$b = -20$

Substituting $b = -20$ into the equation of L, we get:

$\dfrac{x}{5} - \dfrac{y}{20} = 1 \Rightarrow 4x - y - 20 = 0$

The line K is given by $\dfrac{x}{c} + \dfrac{y}{3} = 1$.

Since K is parallel to L, their slopes must be equal. The slope of L is $4$. The slope of K is $-\dfrac{3}{c}$.
